2
A motivating example

In the first part of this section we work through the explicit example
of computing the invariant

\bar{\iota}(F_{1,1})(\gamma)=\langle\gamma\rangle_{1,1}^{X}

for a compact symplectic manifold
X
and an insertion

\gamma\in H^{2}(X,\C)
. Comparing the geometric Gromov-Witten
computation with the formula (
9.2
) for
\bar{\iota}(F_{1,1})

provides geometric intuition for the algebraic constructions of CEI
theory that will be developed later in the paper.

In the second part we give an outline of the algebraic generalizations
of the constructions used in the above example. This outline frames
the remainder of the paper.

2.1
String vertices

We will work with moduli spaces
M_{g,k,l}
parametrizing Riemann
surfaces of genus
g
with
k+l
marked points,
k
of which are
designated as inputs and the rest as outputs. Just like the ordinary
Deligne-Mumford moduli spaces of curves, the spaces
M_{g,k,l}
admit
compactifications
{\overline{M}}_{g,k,l}
parametrizing stable curves.

We will also need a framed version
M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr}
of
M_{g,k,l}
: its
points parametrize the same Riemann surfaces as before, but around
each marked point a small disk has been excised, and a framing of the
boundary circle thus created has been fixed by marking a distinguished
point on this circle. One can think of a framing around a point
P

as fixing (up to homotopy) the complex structure of a small
neighborhood of
P
on the original surface. (We use the fact that
the moduli space of holomorphic structures on an annulus with framed
boundary is homotopic to
S^{1}
.) There is a natural forgetful map

M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr}\rightarrow M_{g,k,l}
which is an
(S^{1})^{k+l}
-fibration.

The homotopy type of the complement, inside
{\overline{M}}_{g,k,l}
, of an

\varepsilon
-tubular neighborhood of the boundary does not depend on

\varepsilon
as long as it is small enough. This complement defines
a (non-closed) rational singular chain
{\mathcal{V}}_{g,k,l}
on
M_{g,k,l}
,
unique up to homotopy, which will be called the string vertex of type

(g,k,l)
. The chains
{\mathcal{V}}_{g,k,l}
were first introduced in physics
by Sen-Zwiebach
[
SZ94
]
; a precise mathematical definition of
them was given by Costello
[
Cos09
]
. (To be precise, the string
vertex
{\mathcal{V}}_{g,k,l}
will be constructed as an

(S^{1})^{k+l}
-equivariant chain on
M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr}
. We will largely
ignore this distinction in the informal discussion in this section.)

For our example we need to understand
{\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2}
and

{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1,0}
. All string vertices are defined recursively starting
from
{\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2}
, which is taken to be one-half the
fundamental class of
M_{0,1,2}={\mathsf{pt}}
, a closed chain in

C_{0}(M_{0,1,2})
. (The coefficient
1/2
is not important at this
point, but the reason for it is the fact that the two outputs of

M_{0,1,2}
are symmetrized.)

To define
{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1,0}
we use the natural operator

\Delta:C_{0}(M_{0,1,2})\rightarrow C_{1}(M_{1,1,0})

which twist-sews the two marked outputs of a Riemann surface
of type
(0,1,2)
. The twist-sew operation cuts small disks around the
output markings, frames the resulting boundary circles, and sews them
in all the
S^{1}
possible ways to do this without necessarily aligning
the framings. Note that the choice of framing does not
matter in the end.

It is easy to visualise
\Delta({\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2})
. It is a closed 1-chain
in
M_{1,1,0}
whose points parametrize those elliptic curves which,
in the hyperbolic metric, have a shortest geodesic of length a fixed
small positive number (the circumference of the small disks that were
excised from the sphere
\C\mathbb{P}^{1}
before twist-sewing). When this
circumference is sufficiently small,
\Delta({\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2})
will be a
small circle surrounding the cusp point
\infty
in

{\overline{M}}_{1,1,0}=S^{2}
. (We ignore the fact that these moduli spaces
have non-trivial stack structure, as we are only interested in their
rational chains/homology.) See the figure below.

The crucial observation, due to Costello, is that
\Delta({\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2})

bounds a unique (up to homotopy) chain

{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1,0}\in C_{2}(M_{1,1,0})
. In other words, the equation

\partial({\mathcal{V}}_{1,1,0})+\Delta({\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2})=0

admits a unique
solution
{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1,0}
. (The above is a particular case of the
quantum master equation that will be used later to define all
string vertices.) As mentioned earlier,
{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1,0}
is the
complement of the small tubular neighborhood
D
(in this case, a
disk) of the boundary
\partial{\overline{M}}_{1,1,0}=\{\infty\}
in

{\overline{M}}_{1,1,0}
.

2.2
Field theories

There are two field theories associated to a symplectic manifold

X
. One is the cohomological field theory (CohFT) of
X
defined
by Kontsevich-Manin
[
KM95
]
. The other is a (conjectural)
geometric TCFT constructed from the Fukaya category of
X
. We need
to understand the relationship between these two theories.

In the CohFT approach we associate to an insertion
\gamma\in H^{2}(X)

the closed differential 2-form
\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma)
on
{\overline{M}}_{1,1}

defined by

\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma)=\pi_{*}(\ev_{1}^{*}\gamma).

Here
\pi
and
\ev_{1}
are the forgetful maps from
{\overline{M}}_{1,1}(X)
to

{\overline{M}}_{1,1}
and to
X
, respectively; the push-forward map

\pi_{*}
uses the virtual fundamental class of
{\overline{M}}_{1,1}(X)
. The
Gromov-Witten invariant
\langle\gamma\rangle_{1,1}^{X}
is recovered
by integrating
\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma)
along
{\overline{M}}_{1,1}
:

\langle\gamma\rangle_{1,1}^{X}=\int_{{\overline{M}}_{1,1}}\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma).

(We ignore the role of the homology class
\beta\in H_{2}(X)
, which is
incorporated in the Novikov ring
\Lambda
.)

A standard (folklore) conjecture asserts that, in parallel to the
construction of this CohFT, one should be able to construct a
geometric TCFT from the Fukaya category of
X
. Its state space
V

should be the dg-vector space of Floer cochains on
X
, whose
cohomology is identified with
H^{*}(X,\C)\otimes\Lambda
.

This geometric TCFT should contain information closely related to that
provided by the CohFT. In particular, one of its structure maps

C_{*}(M_{1,1,0})\otimes V\rightarrow\Lambda

should compute, for
\alpha\in C_{*}(M_{1,1,0})
, the integral

\int_{\alpha}\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma).

(We ignore in this part of the discussion the distinction between
framed and unframed moduli spaces. To be correct, the above structure
map should take as input chains in the
framed
moduli space.)

Note that
a priori

\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma)
is not defined when

\gamma
is not closed; nevertheless, by abuse of notation we will
denote the value of the above TCFT map by

\int_{\alpha}\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma)
even when
\gamma
is not closed.

Summarizing the above discussion, the main differences between the
TCFT and the CohFT are:

–

in the TCFT we allow insertions (forms)
\gamma
that
are not closed, while in the CohFT we only allow closed ones
(cohomology classes);

–

in the CohFT we are allowed to integrate along chains in

{\overline{M}}_{1,1}
; the TCFT only allows chains in
M_{1,1}
.

2.3
The main computation

Our goal is to express the CohFT computation

\langle\gamma\rangle_{1,1}^{X}=\int_{{\overline{M}}_{1,1}}\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma)

in terms of integrals available in the TCFT. As a first step we
decompose the above integral as

\int_{{\overline{M}}_{1,1,0}}\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma)=\int_{{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1,0}}\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma)+\int_{D}\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma).

The first term in this integral is readily available in the TCFT: the
chain
{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}
is entirely contained in the uncompactified moduli
space
M_{1,1}
.

The second term can not be directly expressed in terms of the TCFT; in
fact its value is not determined by the TCFT alone. Nevertheless, the
TCFT does assign a value to integrals of the form

\int_{\partial D}\Omega_{1,1}(\rho)

for 1-forms
\rho
on
X
, possibly non-closed. If we assume that the
TCFT compactifies (i.e., it can be extended to include integrals along
chains in
{\overline{M}}_{1,1}
), Stokes’ theorem implies that for a 1-form

\rho
we will have

\int_{\partial D}\Omega_{1,1}(\rho)=\int_{D}d\Omega_{1,1}(\rho)=\int_{D}\Omega_{1,1}(d\rho).

Let
P
and
Q
denote the operators
V\rightarrow\Lambda
obtained by
integrating a form along
D
and
\partial D
, respectively. Then the
above equality can be written (up to sign) as

Q=[d,P].

In other words, if the TCFT compactifies, then the operator
Q
(part
of the uncompactified TCFT structure) must be homotopy-trivial; the
choice of
P
(which only makes sense in the compactified TCFT) must
be a null-homotopy trivializing
Q
.

Why should we expect the analogue of the operator
Q
to be homotopy
trivial in the setting we are interested in, where the TCFT’s circle
action has been trivialized? For arbitrary
(g,k,l)
there is no
reason to expect the analogue of the chain
\partial D
to be homotopy
trivial in the uncompactified space
M_{g,k,l}
(the case

(g,k,l)=(1,1,0)
is misleading). We want to “close it up” by
adding the missing boundary to
M_{g,k,l}
. Nevertheless, we
want to argue that, if the circle action is homotopy trivial on
V
,
the action of
\partial D
on
V
via the TCFT will also be homotopy
trivial.

Note that the chain
\partial D
is not arbitrary; it has been
constructed as
\partial D=\Delta({\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2})
. The operation
\Delta

includes a circle action in it, as it can be decomposed into the
following three operations applied to the 0-chain
{\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2}
:

1.

cut small disks around the marked outputs of a surface
parametrized by
{\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2}
, and choose framings of each
boundary circle;

2.

rotate the framing of one of the two boundary circles
through all
S^{1}
possible positions;

3.

sew the two boundary circles while aligning the markings.

The second operation is the
S^{1}
action applied to one of the
framings. Thus, while the operator
\Delta
on
C_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr})
is not
homotopy trivial in general, its action
Q
on the TCFT will be, under the
assumption that the
S^{1}
action on the TCFT is homotopy trivial.

All that is left at this point is to construct, explicitly, a homotopy
trivialization for
\Delta({\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2})
from a given trivialization
of the circle action. What we will do, in fact, is to give a formula
for a homotopy trivialization
H
of the composition
\Omega
of the
actions of operations 2 and 3 above. The TCFT action of these two
operations is given by

\Omega(x,y)=\langle Bx,y\rangle

where
B
denotes the circle action on
V
and the pairing is the
Mukai pairing on Hochschild chains, see (
3.4
).
If
B
is trivialized by a splitting
s
, an explicit formula for a
trivialization
H
of
\Omega
is given in
Proposition
7.5
.

The formula (
9.2
) for
\bar{\iota}(F_{1,1})
expresses
precisely the above decomposition:

\bar{\iota}(F_{1,1})=\includegraphics[trim=-28.45274pt 28.45274pt 0.0pt 0.0pt,scale={.4}]{png/110.png}.

The first term denotes the integral along
{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1,0}
(the dot
denotes the string vertex of genus 1, with one input and zero
outputs), while the second one is the result of integrating along

{\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2}
(again, the vertex denotes the string vertex of genus 0,
with one input and two outputs), and applying to the result the
operator
H
which trivializes
\Omega
(the closing of the loop).

Note that it is not
a priori
clear that every extension of a
given TCFT to a compactified one comes from a choice of trivialization
of the circle action. However, results in

[
KS09
,
Cos09
,
Dru14
,
DSV13
,
DSV15
,
KMS13
]
and especially recent results
in
[
Des22
]
suggest that this indeed is the case, so our approach to
constructing the Gromov-Witten compactified TCFT from the
uncompactified one and a trivialization of the circle action is justified.

2.4
Algebraic generalization

The entire discussion above admits an algebraic interpretation in
terms of chasing certain special Maurer-Cartan elements in the
following diagram of differential graded Lie algebras (DGLAs)

\begin{diagram}

Both the abstract construction of CEI and the explicit formulas for
them stated in Theorem
1.3
can be deduced from this
diagram.

We now give a brief description of the DGLAs and the maps between them
in the above diagram; the construction of this diagram is essentially
the contents of the paper. The algebras in the top row of this diagram
are already recognizable from Costello’s work
[
Cos09
]
. The
contribution of this paper is the construction of the quasi-equivalent
second row, which is explicitly computable; in particular we give
explicit formulas for the quasi-isomorphisms
{\mathcal{K}}
and
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}
which
are new.

Costello constructed the DGLA
{\mathfrak{g}}
from singular (topological) chains
on the moduli spaces of curves
M_{g,0,n}
. This DGLA does not depend
on either the algebra
A
or the splitting
s
. (In physics,
Sen-Zwiebach call this
background independent
field theory.) By
contrast,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
and its abelianization
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
are constructed
from symmetric powers of Hochschild chains of the algebra
A
. These
algebras do depend on
A
, but not on the splitting
s
. The map

{\mathcal{K}}
, which is a quasi-isomorphism, can only be constructed in the
presence of
s
.

Costello proved that in
{\mathfrak{g}}
there is a distinguished solution of the
Maurer-Cartan equation, the so-called string vertex
{\mathcal{V}}
. Its
component in
C_{*}(M_{g,0,n})
is precisely the string vertex

{\mathcal{V}}_{g,0,n}
that has appeared earlier in this section. Up to homotopy
the string vertex
{\mathcal{V}}
does not depend on any choices whatsoever.

If the dotted arrow were made explicit (and, conceptually, the
difficulty in constructing it, and CEI as a whole, is related to the
problem of extending a positive-boundary closed TCFT to an arbitrary
one) then the image
\beta^{A}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
would carry precisely
the same information as Costello’s
abstract
Gromov-Witten
potential. Intuitively,
\beta^{A}
carries the information of the TCFT
integrals along the string vertices (for all possible insertions),
i.e., one can compute from it terms similar to the integrals

\int_{{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1,0}}\Omega_{1,1}(\gamma)
and

\int_{{\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,2}}\Omega_{0,3}(\gamma)
that have appeared in the
calculation of the
(1,1)
-invariant.

This abstract potential
\beta^{A}
, which depends only on the algebra

A
, is a special solution of the (complicated) quantum master
equation in
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
. However,
\beta_{A}
is not naturally a power
series of Hochschild (or cyclic) homology classes, as we would like
the CEI to be. To obtain the desired type of invariants we need to
use a splitting
s
in order to further trivialize the DGLA
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
. We
set the bracket of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
to zero in
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
and use
s
to
construct the formality quasi-isomorphism
{\mathcal{K}}:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
.
The element
{\mathcal{K}}(\beta_{A})
, which by definition is a solution of the
Maurer-Cartan equation in
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
, becomes precisely an element
in the homology of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
. This is precisely where we want the
descendent CEI potential
{\mathcal{D}}^{A,s}
to live, so it is reasonable to
take
{\mathcal{D}}^{A,s}={\mathcal{K}}(\beta_{A})
.

The problem with this approach is that to the best of our knowledge
there is no way to make the dotted map in the diagram explicit. (This
problem was recognized by Costello, and his work involves a great deal
of effort to circumvent this issue in a non-constructive way.) We
solve this difficulty by replacing all the DGLAs above by Koszul
resolutions of themselves, denoted by
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
, etc. The
horizontal maps in the second row of the diagram can now be made
explicitly computable. This involves further replacing the DGLA

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
, which is still built from singular chains on moduli spaces, by
a DGLA constructed from ribbon graphs, following ideas of
Egas
[
Ega15
]
. This yields the combinatorial comparison
quasi-isomorphism
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf comb}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
. Note that even though the
vertical Koszul resolution maps
\bar{\iota}
are quasi-isomorphisms, their
inverses are not explicit and this prevents one from defining the
dotted map
{\mathfrak{g}}\dashrightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
.

In practice we completely disregard the left vertical of the diagram.
Because both maps
{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf comb}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
are
quasi-isomorphisms, Costello’s theorem tells us that, up to homotopy,
there is a unique solution to the Maurer-Cartan equation in

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf comb}
, and by uniqueness this must be the image of the original
string vertex
{\mathcal{V}}
. We call this solution the combinatorial string
vertex
{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf comb}
; it can be computed directly in

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf comb}
, without reference to
{\mathfrak{g}}
or
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
. By the commutativity
of the diagram, the image
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}(\rho^{A}({\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}))\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
is

\bar{\iota}({\mathcal{D}})
. Breaking it down by genus and number of insertions
gives the desired definition of
\bar{\iota}(F_{g,n}^{A,s})
.

2.5
Plan of the paper

Section
3
collects preliminaries regarding complexes with
pairing and circle action, partly in order to fix notations. In
Section
4
we present constructions of the DGLAs
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}

and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
associated with the
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
. In
Section
5
we present constructions of the
Sen-Zwiebach DGLA
{\mathfrak{g}}
and its Koszul resolution
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
, as well as of
the combinatorial version
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf comb}
of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
. We introduce the
notion of (combinatorial) string vertices, which are the components of
the unique solution of the Maurer-Cartan equation in these DGLAs.
Section
6
gives a detailed account of the definition of
categorical enumerative invariants (CEI) following the ideas
above. Logically this definition relies on Lemma
7.2

proved in the subsequent section
7
.

Sections
7
and
8
deal with the
construction, in terms of a splitting
s
, of the
L_{\infty}

trivializing maps
{\mathcal{K}}
and
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}
. In the final section
(Section
9
) we obtain the Feynman sum formulas for CEI
described in Theorem
1.3
.

3
Preliminaries

3.1
Notations and conventions

Unless otherwise stated, all our vector spaces will be
\Z/2\Z
-graded
over a field
\mathbb{K}
of characteristic zero. (In the
\Z
-graded case
we obtain an analogue of the dimension axiom for Gromov-Witten theory
in Appendix
A
.) Nevertheless, we think of our
chain complexes as being
homologically
graded: differentials
decrease degree. For a
\Z
-graded vector space
V=\oplus_{n}V_{n}
we
use the homological grading convention: the shift
V[k]
is the graded
vector space whose
n
-th graded piece is
V_{n-k}
. In the

\Z/2\Z
-graded case we shall still use the same notation
[k]
even
though only the parity of
k
matters.

Secondly, we shall always use the shifted sign conventions described
in
[
Cho08
,
She15
]
. The shifted degree
|x|^{\prime}
of an element

x
is defined to be

|x|^{\prime}=|x|+1.

All the operations in cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras, including the
pairing, and in
L_{\infty}
-algebras, including DGLAs, differ by the
sign

(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n-k)|x_{k}|}

from the usual ones when applied to a tensor

x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{n}
. In particular a Maurer-Cartan element
in a DGLA has shifted degree zero.

Thirdly, we shall use
sign diagrams
as introduced by
Sheridan
[
She15
, Appendix C]
to deal with signs involved when
composing multi-linear maps. For example, a multi-linear map
\varphi

with
2
inputs and
3
outputs will be drawn as:

The extra red line has degree
|\varphi|
. As explained in
loc. cit.
, adding the red line makes the total degrees around the
vertex even, which allows one to move around vertices in a sign
diagram. In other words, signs are invariant under isotopies of sign
diagrams.

3.2
Representations of annuli

We shall frequently use complexes with circle action, together with a
compatible pairing on them. From the TCFT point of view these are
precisely the same thing as the representations of the sub-PROP of
annuli (see
[
Cos09
]
and Section
5
).

A circle action on a chain complex
(C,\partial)
is given by an
operator
\delta:C\rightarrow C
of degree one, satisfying
\delta^{2}=0
and

[\partial,\delta]=\partial\delta+\delta\partial=0
. Associated with
a chain complex with circle action we set

\displaystyle C^{\mathsf{Tate}}

\displaystyle=\big(C\!\left(\hskip-2.33336pt\hbox{$\left(\hbox{$u$}\right)$}\hskip-2.33336pt\right)\!\hskip 0.66666pt,\partial+u\delta\big)

\displaystyle C_{+}

\displaystyle=\big(C\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$u$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt,\partial+u\delta\big)

\displaystyle C_{-}

\displaystyle=C^{\mathsf{Tate}}/C_{+}=\big(C[u^{-1}],\partial+u\delta\big),

where
u
is a formal variable of even degree. Observe that there is
a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow u\cdot C_{+}\rightarrow C^{\mathsf{Tate}}\rightarrow C_{-}\rightarrow 0.

For a given chain complex
(C,\partial)
the shift
C[1]
is endowed
with the differential
-\partial
. For example, the differential of
the complex
C_{+}[1]
is given by
-\partial-u\delta
. To avoid
confusions, we shall reserve the notation
\eth
for all circle
equivariant boundary maps. For example, when considering the

\Hom
-complex
\Hom(L_{+}[1],L_{-})
we put the differential

f\mapsto[\eth,f]=(\partial+u\delta)f-(-1)^{|f|}f\big(-(\partial+u\delta)\big)=(\partial+u\delta)f+(-1)^{|f|}f(\partial+u\delta).

A pairing on a chain complex with circle action is a symmetric
bilinear chain map of even degree

\langle\,-\,,\,-\,\rangle:C\otimes C\rightarrow\mathbb{K}

such that the circle operator
\delta
is self-adjoint, i.e., we have

\langle\delta x,y\rangle=(-1)^{|x|}\langle x,\delta y\rangle\mbox{ for all }x,y\in C.

A pairing like the one described above induces a so-called
higher
residue pairing
on the associated Tate complex, with values in

\mathbb{K}\!\left(\hskip-2.33336pt\hbox{$\left(\hbox{$u$}\right)$}\hskip-2.33336pt\right)\!\hskip 0.66666pt
, defined for
x=\sum x_{k}\cdot u^{k}
and

y=\sum y_{l}\cdot u^{l}
by

\langle x,\,y\rangle_{\sf hres}=\sum_{k,l}(-1)^{l}\langle x_{k},\,y_{l}\rangle\cdot u^{k+l}.

Its residue at
u=\infty
1
1

1

The choice of taking residue at

u=\infty
instead of
u=0
(corresponding to
k+l=-1
) is motivated
by our desire for homogeneity in the construction of the Weyl
algebra later in (
3.3
). Although there might
be a more conceptual reason for this related to the Fourier-Laplace
transform of Hodge structures
[
Sab08
]
.
is called the residue
pairing, valued in
\mathbb{K}
and defined by

\langle x\cdot u^{k},y\cdot u^{l}\rangle_{\sf res}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{l}\langle x,y\rangle\;\;\;&\mbox{if}\;\;k+l=1\\
0\;\;\;&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Due to the sign
(-1)^{l}
the residue pairing
\langle\,-\,,\,-\,\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}
is
anti-symmetric. It is an even pairing since the original pairing is even.

3.3
Weyl algebra and Fock spaces

We now review the relationship, discovered by Costello
[
Cos09
]
,
between Batalin-Vilkovisky (BV) algebras and Fock spaces for dg-vector
spaces with circle action and pairing. This builds on earlier work of
Givental
[
Giv01
]
.

Let
(C,\partial,\delta,\langle\,-\,,\,-\,\rangle)
be a complex with circle action and
pairing, as describe above. Associated with the symplectic vector
space
(C^{\mathsf{Tate}},\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathsf{res}})
is the Weyl algebra

{\mathcal{W}}\big(C^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)
defined as

{\mathcal{W}}\big(C^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)=T\big(C^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt/\big(\alpha\otimes\beta-(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}\beta\otimes\alpha=\hbar\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\sf res}\big).

Here, again, the formal variable
\hbar
is of even degree. Note that
the relation is homogeneous with respect to the
\Z/2\Z
-grading.

The positive subspace
u\cdot C_{+}
is a subcomplex of
C^{\mathsf{Tate}}
.
Hence the left ideal
\big(u\cdot C_{+}\big)_{L}
generated by this
subspace is a dg-ideal in
{\mathcal{W}}(C^{\mathsf{Tate}})
. The quotient

\mbox{${\mathscr{F}}$}={\mathcal{W}}\big(C^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)/\big(u\cdot C_{+}\big)_{L}

is known as the Fock space of
{\mathcal{W}}(C^{\mathsf{Tate}})
; it is a left dg-module
of the Weyl algebra.

The linear subspace
C_{-}
of
C^{\mathsf{Tate}}
, on the other hand, is
not
a subcomplex: the differential
\partial+u\delta
of an element

u^{0}x
is by definition equal to
u^{0}\partial(x)
when computed in
C_{-}
,
but it equals
u^{0}\partial(x)+u^{1}\delta(x)
in
C^{\mathsf{Tate}}
.

Nevertheless, disregarding differentials,
C_{-}
is still isotropic as
a graded vector subspace of the symplectic space
(C^{\mathsf{Tate}},\langle\,-\,,\,-\,\rangle_{\mathsf{res}})
. This yields an embedding of algebras (without
differentials!)

\Sym(C_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{W}}\big(C^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big).

Post-composing with the canonical projection to the Fock space yields
an isomorphism of graded vector spaces

\mbox{$\Sym$}(C_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathscr{F}}.

3.4
Main example

The main example of a chain complex with circle action and pairing is
provided by the shifted reduced Hochschild chain complex of a cyclic
unital
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
,

L=C_{*}(A)[d].

(Any
d\in\Z
will work, though in practice if
A
is a cyclic

A_{\infty}
-algebra we take
d
to be its Calabi-Yau dimension.) Its
homology is the shifted Hochschild homology of
A
,

H=H_{*}(L)=HH_{*}(A)[d].

Using the cyclic pairing and condition
(\dagger)

in (
1.2
)
H
is isomorphic to the dual of the Hochschild
cohomology of
A
, also known as
HH^{*}(A,A^{*})
. The degree of an
element
x_{0}[x_{1}|\ldots|x_{n}]\in L
is
\sum_{k=1}^{n}|x_{k}|^{\prime}+(d-1)

with the extra
-1
being due to the fact that
x_{0}
is not shifted in
defining
L
. The circle action is given by the Connes operator
B
,
and the symmetric pairing on
L
is the chain-level Mukai pairing

\langle\,-\,,\,-\,\rangle_{\sf Muk}:L\otimes L\rightarrow\mathbb{K}.

(The chain-level Mukai pairing is given by the action on the TCFT of the
unique homology class in
H_{0}(M_{0,2,0})
. An explicit
formula for it can be found in
[
She15
, Definition 5.19]
.) As described in
the previous section, if we denote by
{\mathscr{F}}_{A}
the Fock space
associated with the quadruple
(L,b,B,\langle-,-\rangle_{\sf Muk})
then
we have a natural isomorphism of graded vector spaces

\mbox{$\Sym$}(L_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathscr{F}}_{A}.

We shall use this isomorphism to define a DGLA structure on

\Sym(L_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt
in the next section.

4
Two DGLAs associated to a representation of annuli

Throughout this section we continue to work with a cyclic

A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
satisfying condition
(\dagger)
in
in (
1.2
). We also work with the notations
L
,
L_{-}
,

L_{+}
and
L^{\mathsf{Tate}}
as introduced in (
3.4
). We
will associate to this data the two DGLAs
{\mathfrak{h}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
described
in (
2.4
) and construct the quasi-isomorphism

\bar{\iota}
.

4.1
A first DGLA associated with
A

Costello
[
Cos09
]
observed that under the isomorphism

\mbox{$\Sym$}(C_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathscr{F}}.

in (
3.3
) the differential on the Fock space pulls back
to a differential on the symmetric algebra
\Sym L_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt

of a special form. It can be written as
b+uB+\hbar\Delta
for a BV
differential
\Delta
, which means that
\Delta
is a degree one,
square zero, second order differential operator of the symmetric
algebra. As such it is uniquely determined by its action on

\Sym^{\leq 2}L_{-}
; moreover, it vanishes on
\Sym^{\leq 1}L_{-}
, and
on
\Sym^{2}L_{-}
it is explicitly given by the formula

\Delta(x\cdot y)=\Omega(x,y)=\langle Bx_{0},y_{0}\rangle,

for elements
x,y\in L_{-}
of the form

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=x_{0}+x_{-1}u^{-1}+x_{-2}u^{-2}+\cdots,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=y_{0}+y_{-1}u^{-1}+y_{-2}u^{-2}+\cdots.

Getzler
[
Get94
]
proved that a BV differential such as
\Delta

induces a DGLA structure on the shifted vector space

(\Sym L_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt[1]
. More precisely, we define a DGLA
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}

whose graded vector space is

{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}=\Sym(L_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt[1]

(with another even formal variable
\lambda
added) and with
differential
b+uB+\hbar\Delta
, extended
\lambda
-linearly. The Lie
bracket, which by our convention (
3.1
) is a symmetric
map

\{-,-\}:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]\otimes{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1],

is defined to be the failure of
\Delta
to be a derivation:

\{x,y\}=\Delta(x\cdot y)-(\Delta x\cdot y)-(-1)^{|x|}(x\cdot\Delta y).

Since
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]=\Sym(L_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt[2]
, the
degree
|x|
here is computed in
\Sym(L_{-})
.

4.2
A second DGLA associated with
A

There is another DGLA associated with the
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
which
we now construct. As a
\Z/2\Z
-graded vector space it is given by

\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}=\bigoplus_{k\geq 1,l}\Hom^{\mathsf{c}}\big(\Sym^{k}(L_{+}[1]),\Sym^{l}(L_{-})\big)\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt.

Here the superscript “
\mathsf{c}
” denotes continuous homomorphisms in
the
u
-adic topology. To simplify the notation we will write

L_{k,l}=\Hom^{\sf c}\left(\Sym^{k}(L_{+}[1]),\Sym^{l}(L_{-})\right).

The degree of an element
\Phi\in L_{k,l}
will be denoted by

|\Phi|
.

As in (
3.2
), in order to avoid confusion about signs
we shall use the notation
\eth
for the differential induced from

b+uB
. For example, for an element
\Phi\in L_{k,l}
, the differential

\eth
acts as

\eth\Phi=(b+uB)\Phi+(-1)^{|\Phi|}\Phi(b+uB).

Note that this is indeed the commutator action, due to the shift
[1]

of
L_{+}
.

To define
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
we need to define its differential and bracket.
We begin with the former, which will be of the form

\eth+\hbar\Delta+\iota
for a BV operator
\Delta
and a new
“input-output” operator
\iota
which we now define.

First, the BV operator
\Delta
in the previous subsection can be
extended to
\Delta:L_{k,l}\rightarrow L_{k,l-2}
by simply setting

\Delta(\Phi)=\Delta\circ\Phi,\;\;\;\mbox{for}\;\;\Phi\in L_{k,l}.

One easily verifies that
(\eth+\hbar\Delta)^{2}=0
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
.

For an element
\beta\in L_{+}[1]
we define a contraction operator

C_{\beta}:\Sym^{l}L_{-}\rightarrow\Sym^{l-1}L_{-}
by

C_{\beta}\big(\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{l}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{l}(-1)^{|\alpha_{i}|(|\alpha_{1}|+\cdots+|\alpha_{i-1}|)}\langle u\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}\cdot\alpha_{1}\cdots\widehat{\alpha_{i}}\cdots\alpha_{l}

Lemma 4.1
.

The following identities hold:

(a)

C_{\beta}
is a derivation of
\Sym L_{-}
of degree

|\beta|^{\prime}
, i.e. we have

C_{\beta}(\alpha\cdot\gamma)=C_{\beta}(\alpha)\cdot\gamma+(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|^{\prime}}\alpha\cdot C_{\beta}(\gamma)

(b)

C_{\beta}\circ\Delta-(-1)^{|\beta|^{\prime}}\Delta\circ C_{\beta}=0
.

(c)

C_{\beta_{1}}C_{\beta_{2}}-(-1)^{|\beta_{1}|^{\prime}|\beta_{2}|^{\prime}}C_{\beta_{2}}C_{\beta_{1}}=0
.

(d)

[b+uB,C_{\beta}]=-C_{\eth\beta}
with
\eth\beta=-(b+uB)\beta
.

Proof.

Direct calculation.
∎

We now use these contraction operators to construct one more
Koszul-type differential
\iota
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
. On individual components

L_{k,l}
this is the odd map
\iota:L_{k,l}\rightarrow L_{k+1,l-1}
defined by

\iota(\Phi)(\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k+1})=\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}(-1)^{|\beta_{j}|(|\beta_{1}|+\cdots+|\beta_{j-1}|+|\Phi|)}C_{\beta_{j}}\Phi(\beta_{1}\cdots\widehat{\beta_{j}}\cdots\beta_{k+1})

Graphically, this definition is presented as the following sign diagram

(See
[
She15
]
for details on sign diagrams.)

Lemma 4.2
.

We have
(\eth+\iota+\hbar\Delta)^{2}=0
.

Proof.

With the explicit formula for
\iota
one checks
directly that
[\eth,\iota]=[\iota,\iota]=[\iota,\Delta]=0
. The proof
is again by direct calculation and will be omitted.
∎

Denote by
\Theta:L_{-}\rightarrow L_{+}[1]
the circle action map defined by

\Theta(\alpha)=B(\alpha_{0})

for
\alpha=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{-1}u^{-1}+\cdots\in L_{-}
. Note that

\Theta
is a map of chain map of degree zero because of the shift
[1]
:

\Theta\eth(\alpha)=Bb\alpha_{0}=-bB\alpha_{0}=\eth\Theta(\alpha).

For every natural number
r\geq 1
we use
\Theta
to define a
composition operation

\circ_{r}:L_{k_{2},l_{2}}\otimes L_{k_{1},l_{1}}\rightarrow L_{k_{1}+k_{2}-r,l_{1}+l_{2}-r},\mbox{ for }l_{1},k_{2}\geq r

as follows. For a symmetric tensor
\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{n}
and a
subset
A=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{s}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
with

a_{1}<\cdots<a_{s}
we write

\gamma_{A}=\gamma_{a_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{a_{s}}.

With this notation we define

(\Psi\circ_{r}\Phi)(\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k_{1}+k_{2}-r})\!=\!\sum_{P,Q}\sum_{I,J}\epsilon_{I,J}\epsilon_{P,Q}(-1)^{|\Psi||\Phi(\beta_{I})_{P}|}\Phi(\beta_{I})_{P}\cdot\Psi\big(\Theta^{\otimes r}(\Phi(\beta_{I})_{Q})\otimes\beta_{J}\big).

The summation is over all shuffles
I\coprod J=\{1,\ldots,k_{1}+k_{2}-r\}

of type
(k_{1},k_{2}-r)
and
P\coprod Q=\{1,\ldots,l_{1}\}
of type

(r,l_{1}-r)
. The signs
\epsilon_{I,J}
and
\epsilon_{P,Q}
are the
Koszul signs associated with the permutations given by the shuffles.
The following sign diagram illustrate this definition in the case with

k_{1}=3
,
l_{1}=5
,
k_{2}=4
,
l_{2}=3
and
r=2
:

The black dots in the middle arrows indicate that we apply the map

\Theta
at each strand.

Note that the composition defined above is
not
an associative
operation. Nevertheless, we shall make use of its commutators to
define a Lie bracket structure on
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
. Indeed, we define a
symmetric map
\{-,-\}_{\hbar}:\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]\otimes\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]

by putting

\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{\hbar}=(-1)^{|\Psi|}\sum_{r\geq 1}\big(\Psi\circ_{r}\Phi-(-1)^{|\Psi||\Phi|}\Phi\circ_{r}\Psi\big)\hbar^{r-1}.

The degrees
|\Phi|
and
|\Psi|
are computed in
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
. The extra
sign
(-1)^{|\Psi|}
is due to our convention to use shifted signs in
DGLAs.

Theorem 4.3
.

The triple
\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A},\eth+\iota+\hbar\Delta,\{-,-\}_{\hbar}\big)
forms a DGLA.

Proof.

One can verify directly that
\eth
satisfies the following Leibniz
property against the Lie bracket
\{-,-\}_{\hbar}
:

\eth\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{\hbar}+\{\eth\Psi,\Phi\}_{\hbar}+(-1)^{|\Psi|^{\prime}}\{\Psi,\eth\Phi\}_{\hbar}=0.

Note that neither
\iota
nor
\Delta
are derivations
of the Lie bracket. Nevertheless, their sum

\iota+\hbar\Delta

is
a derivation, that is

(\iota+\hbar\Delta)\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{\hbar}+\{(\iota+\hbar\Delta)\Psi,\Phi\}_{\hbar}+(-1)^{|\Psi|^{\prime}}\{\Psi,(\iota+\hbar\Delta)\Phi\}_{\hbar}=0.

To prove this equation, in the case when the power of
\hbar
is zero,
we want to prove that

\iota\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{1}+\{\iota\Psi,\Phi\}_{1}+(-1)^{|\Psi|^{\prime}}\{\Psi,\iota\Phi\}_{1}=0.

For this consider the following sign diagram:

The two diagrams are equal by isotopy invariance. The left hand
side appears in
\iota\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{1}
with the sign
(-1)^{|\Psi|}
,
while the right hand side appears in
\{\iota\Psi,\Phi\}_{1}
with the
sign
(-1)^{|\Psi|^{\prime}}
. This shows that the two terms cancel out.

For general
r\geq 2
we want to prove that

\displaystyle\iota\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{r}+

\displaystyle\{\iota\Psi,\Phi\}_{r}+(-1)^{|\Psi|^{\prime}}\{\Psi,\iota\Phi\}_{r}=

\displaystyle-\Delta\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{r-1}-\{\Delta\Psi,\Phi\}_{r-1}-(-1)^{|\Psi|^{\prime}}\{\Psi,\Delta\Phi\}_{r-1}.

This is illustrated in the following sign diagram:

The left hand side appears in
\{\iota\Psi,\Phi\}_{r}
with the
sign
(-1)^{|\Psi|^{\prime}}
, while the right hand side appears in
-\Delta\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{r-1}
with the same sign. Other cancellations are
similarly proved.

It remains to prove the Jacobi identity, that for three elements

\Psi,\Phi,\Upsilon\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
we have

\{\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{\hbar},\Upsilon\}_{\hbar}+(-1)^{|\Upsilon|^{\prime}|\Phi|^{\prime}}\{\{\Psi,\Upsilon\}_{\hbar},\Phi\}_{\hbar}+(-1)^{|\Psi|^{\prime}|\Phi|^{\prime}+|\Psi|^{\prime}|\Upsilon|^{\prime}}\{\{\Phi,\Upsilon\}_{\hbar},\Psi\}_{\hbar}=0

There are in total 12 types of compositions in the above sum which
cancel out in pairs. For example, consider the following sign diagram:

The left hand side appears in
\{\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{\hbar},\Upsilon\}_{\hbar}

with the sign
(-1)^{|\Psi|}(-1)^{|\Psi|+|\Phi|}=(-1)^{|\Phi|}
, while
the right hand side appears in

(-1)^{|\Psi|^{\prime}|\Phi|^{\prime}+|\Psi|^{\prime}|\Upsilon|^{\prime}}\{\{\Phi,\Upsilon\}_{\hbar},\Psi\}_{\hbar}

with the sign

(-1)^{|\Psi|^{\prime}|\Phi|^{\prime}+|\Psi|^{\prime}|\Upsilon|^{\prime}}(-1)^{|\Upsilon|}(-1)(-1)^{|\Psi||\Phi|+|\Psi||\Upsilon|}=-(-1)^{|\Phi|},

which shows the two terms cancel out. The cancellation of the other
pairs is similar.
∎

4.3
The Koszul resolution map
\bar{\iota}

We now relate the two DGLAs
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
associated with
A
by
constructing a map
\bar{\iota}
between them. To be precise, we will
construct a quasi-isomorphism
\bar{\iota}:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
from a certain
quotient
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}
of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
. The distinction between
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
and

{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}
turns out to be irrelevant, see the discussion after the statement
of Theorem
7.1
.

Denote by

{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}=\left(\Sym^{\geq 1}L_{-}\right)\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt[1]

the strictly positive symmetric powers of
L_{-}
. This is not a
sub-DGLA of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
, since for instance
\Delta(\Sym^{2}L_{-})
would not
be contained in
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}
. However, the scalar part

\mathbb{K}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt[1]
is in the center of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
, implying
that
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}
is a
quotient
of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
, i.e., there is a short
exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathbb{K}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt[1]\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}\rightarrow 0.

Define the map
\bar{\iota}:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
by setting

\bar{\iota}(\alpha)=(-1)^{|\alpha|}\iota(\alpha).

The sign in the definition of
\bar{\iota}
is needed in order to turn the
map
\iota
(which satisfies the anti-commutative property of
Lemma
4.2
) into a map of chain complexes.

Lemma 4.4
.

The morphism
\bar{\iota}
is a quasi-isomorphism of DGLAs.

Proof.

We shall prove that the mapping cone of
\bar{\iota}
is acyclic. For
this it suffices to prove that for each
n\geq 1
the following
sequence of dg vector spaces is exact:

0\rightarrow\Sym^{n}L_{-}\rightarrow\Hom^{\mathsf{c}}(L_{+}[1],\Sym^{n-1}L_{-})\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\Hom^{\mathsf{c}}(\Sym^{n}(L_{+}[1]),\mathbb{C})\rightarrow 0.

The horizontal
arrows are the map
\iota
. We argue that the total complex of this
sequence has zero homology, by considering the spectral sequence
associated with the
u
-filtration. Since
A
satisfies the
degeneration property, the
E^{1}
page is given by

0\rightarrow\Sym^{n}H_{-}\rightarrow\Hom^{\mathsf{c}}(H_{+},\Sym^{n-1}H_{-})\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\Hom^{\mathsf{c}}(\wedge^{n}H_{+},\mathbb{C})\rightarrow 0

where

H=HH_{*}(A)[d]
is the shifted Hochschild homology of
A
. Because
A

is assumed to be smooth
H
is finite dimensional. From this
finiteness condition we can deduce the exactness of the
E^{1}
-page as
follows. Observe that there is an isomorphism for each
k+l=n

I_{k,l}:\Sym^{k}(H_{-}[1])\otimes\Sym^{l}H_{-}\rightarrow\Hom^{\mathsf{c}}(\Sym^{k}(H_{+}[1]),\Sym^{l}H_{-})

defined using the Mukai pairing on

H
. In the case of
k=1
,
l=0
this map is explicitly given by

I_{0,0}(x)(y)={\mathsf{res}}_{u=\infty}\langle x,u\cdot y\rangle.

For general
k
and
l
it is defined similarly. The fact that

I_{k,l}
is an isomorphism follows from a result of
Shklyarov
[
Shk13
]
(following
[
CW10
]
) proving that the
categorical Mukai pairing is non-degenerate when
A
is smooth and
proper. Putting the isomorphisms
I_{k,l}
together we conclude that
the
E^{1}
page is isomorphic to the complex

0\rightarrow\Sym^{n}H_{-}\rightarrow H_{-}\otimes\Sym^{n-1}H_{-}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\Sym^{n}(H_{-}[1])\rightarrow 0

endowed with the usual Koszul differential.
Exactness follows.
∎

5
The Sen-Zwiebach DGLA and string vertices

There are two other DGLAs
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
, constructed in the
topological world of chains on moduli spaces of smooth Riemann
surfaces, whose definitions are completely analogous to those of
{\mathfrak{h}}

and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
in the previous section. The DGLAs
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
should be
thought of as “master” versions of the
{\mathfrak{h}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
associated
with an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
: for any such
A
there are canonical
maps
{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
.

As in the algebraic case we will describe two DGLAs
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
.
In the first one we assume there are no inputs; all outputs are
treated the same. In the the second one we distinguish inputs from
outputs, and we assume that there is at least one input at all
times. The first construction is due to Sen-Zwiebach
[
SZ94
]
.
The second construction is in some sense a “Koszul” resolution of
the first one, and it first appeared in the unpublished work of
Costello and the authors
[
CCT20
]
.

Throughout this section
C_{*}(-)
will denote the functor of
normalized singular chains with coefficients in the ground field

\mathbb{K}
from topological spaces to dg-vector spaces.

5.1
Moduli spaces of curves and sewing operations

For
g\geq 0
and
k,l\geq 0
satisfying
2g-2+k+l>0
we consider the
coarse moduli space
M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr}
of Riemann surfaces of genus
g
,
with
k+l
marked and framed points,
k
of which are designated as
inputs, and the rest as outputs. Explicitly, a point in

M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr}
is given by the data
(\Sigma,p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},q_{1},\ldots,q_{l},\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k},\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{l})
where

•

\Sigma
is a smooth Riemann surface of genus
g
,

•

the
p
’s and
q
’s are distinct marked points on
\Sigma
,

•

the
\varphi
’s and
\psi
’s are framings around the marked
points, given by biholomorphic maps

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}:

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{D}^{2}\rightarrow U_{\epsilon_{i}}(p_{i}),\;\;1\leq i\leq k

\displaystyle\psi_{j}:

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{D}^{2}\rightarrow U_{\epsilon_{j}}(q_{j}),\;\;1\leq j\leq l

where
\mathbb{D}^{2}
stands for the unit disk in
\C
, and

U_{\epsilon}(x)
is a radius
\epsilon
disk of the marked point
x
on

\Sigma
, under, say, the unique hyperbolic metric on

\Sigma\setminus\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},q_{1},\ldots,q_{l}\}
. We require that
the biholomorphic maps
\varphi
and
\psi
extend to an open
neighborhood of
\mathbb{D}^{2}
. We also require that the closures of
all the framed disks be disjoint, i.e., for any two distinct points

x,y\in\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},q_{1},\ldots,q_{l}\}
we have

\overline{U_{\epsilon_{i}}(x)}\cap\overline{U_{\epsilon_{j}}(y)}=\emptyset.

Note that the fibration
M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr}\rightarrow M_{g,k,l}
which forgets the
framing is a homotopy
(S^{1})^{k+l}
-bundle. Indeed, for each index

1\leq i\leq k
or
1\leq j\leq l
, there is a corresponding circle
action on the framing
\varphi_{i}
or
\psi_{j}
by precomposing with the
rotation map
e^{i\theta}:\mathbb{D}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}^{2}
.

We shall make use of different types of sewing maps of Riemann
surfaces:

•

(Sewing inputs and outputs.)

Fix a positive integer
1\leq r\leq l
. There is a sewing map

G_{r}:M_{g^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}^{\sf fr}\times M_{g^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime}}^{\sf fr}\rightarrow M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr}

with
g=g^{\prime}+g^{\prime\prime}+r-1
,
k=k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}-r
,
l=l^{\prime}+l^{\prime\prime}-r
. This map is
defined by removing the first
r
input open disks

\varphi^{\prime}_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{2}),\ldots,\varphi^{\prime}_{r}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
from the first
surface, removing the first
r
output open disks

\psi^{\prime\prime}_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{2}),\ldots,\psi^{\prime\prime}_{r}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
from the
second surface, and then gluing along the boundary circles using the
coordinates from the framings in such a way that
\varphi^{\prime}_{i}(S^{1})
is
identified with
\psi^{\prime\prime}_{i}(S^{1})
. Applying the functor
C_{*}(-)
and
pre-composing with the Alexander-Whitney map yields a composition
map which we denote by

G_{r}:C_{*}(M_{g^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}^{\sf fr})\otimes C_{*}(M_{g^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime}}^{\sf fr})\rightarrow C_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr})

as well.

•

(Sewing two outputs.)

In a similar way, for two indices
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq l^{\prime}
and

1\leq j^{\prime\prime}\leq l^{\prime\prime}
we have a sewing map between two outputs of
two Riemann surfaces:

G_{j^{\prime}j^{\prime\prime}}:C_{*}(M_{g^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}^{\sf fr})\otimes C_{*}(M_{g^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime}}^{\sf fr})\rightarrow C_{*}(M_{g^{\prime}+g^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime}+l^{\prime\prime}-2}^{\sf fr}).

•

(Self-sewing two outputs.)

We can also sew two outputs of a single Riemann surface. Indeed, for
a pair of indices
1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}\leq l
we have a self-sewing
map:

G_{j_{1}j_{2}}:C_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr})\rightarrow C_{*}(M_{g+1,k,l-2}^{\sf fr}).

We have slightly abused the notation for
G
, but no confusion can
arise in this way.

It is worth pointing out that we never sew two inputs together, and we
never self-sew an input to an output. This asymmetry between inputs
and outputs causes considerable difficulty in the theory. However, it
is at essential in order to avoid later computing traces on infinite
dimensional vector spaces!

5.2
The Sen-Zwiebach DGLA

Consider the moduli space
M_{g,0,n}^{\sf fr}
with only outgoing marked
points. Let
C_{*}(M_{g,0,n}^{\sf fr})
be its associated normalized
singular chain complex. Taking the homotopy quotient by the
n

commuting circle actions yields the equivariant chain complex

C_{*}(M_{g,0,n}^{\sf fr})_{(S^{1})^{n}}=\big(C_{*}(M_{g,0,n}^{\sf fr})[u_{1}^{-1},\ldots,u_{n}^{-1}],\eth=\partial+\sum_{j=1}^{n}u_{j}B_{j}\big)

The symmetric group
S_{n}
acts on
C_{*}(M_{g,0,n}^{\sf fr})_{(S^{1})^{n}}

diagonally by the induced action on
C_{*}(M_{g,0,n}^{\sf fr})
and by
permutation of the indices of the
u
-variables. Further taking

S_{n}
coinvariants yields

C_{*}(M_{g,0,n}^{\sf fr})_{\sf hS}=C_{*}(M_{g,0,n}^{\sf fr})_{(S^{1})^{n}\rtimes S_{n}}.

We will now describe a DGLA structure on the super
vector space

{\mathfrak{g}}=\big(\bigoplus_{g,n}C_{*}(M_{g,0,n}^{\sf fr})_{\sf hS}\big)\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt[1].

We shall refer to this DGLA as the Sen-Zwiebach DGLA,
see
[
SZ94
,
Cos09
]
. Its construction is parallel to that of
the DGLA
{\mathfrak{h}}
associated to a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra
in (
4.1
). Indeed, we define an operator

{\mathfrak{g}}[1]\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}[1]
of degree one by setting

\Delta(\alpha)=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}G_{ij}\big(\pi_{ij}(B_{i}\alpha)\big),

where
G_{ij}
is induced from the self-sewing map

M^{\sf fr}_{g,0,n}\rightarrow M_{g+1,0,n-2}^{\sf fr}
, and the map
\pi_{ij}
takes an
equivariant chain
\gamma
to

\pi_{ij}(\gamma)=\gamma\mid_{u^{-1}_{i}=u^{-1}_{j}=0}.

That is,
\pi_{ij}
only keeps the constant term of
\gamma
in the
expansion in the variables
u_{i}^{-1}
and
u_{j}^{-1}
. In other words,
the definition of
\Delta
is such that the sewing operation is
only performed when the circle parameters
u_{i}
and
u_{j}
in
\alpha

are both power zero.

The Lie bracket map, a symmetric degree one map
{\mathfrak{g}}[1]\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}[1]\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}[1]
, is similarly defined. Explicitly, for
\alpha\in C_{*}(M_{g^{\prime},0,n^{\prime}}^{\sf fr})_{\sf hS}
and
\beta\in C_{*}(M_{g^{\prime\prime},0,n^{\prime\prime}}^{\sf fr})_{\sf hS}
,
we set

\{\alpha,\beta\}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n^{\prime}\\
1\leq j\leq n^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}G_{ij}\big(\pi_{i}(B_{i}\alpha),\pi_{j}(\beta)\big).

Theorem 5.1
.

The triple
\big({\mathfrak{g}},\partial+uB+\hbar\Delta,\{-,-\}\big)
forms a DGLA.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of the fact that a dgBV algebra
induces DGLA structure
[
Get94
]
. The commutative algebra structure in
this case is induced from taking disjoint unions of Riemann surfaces.
∎

Note further that the DGLA
{\mathfrak{g}}
admits a homological
\Z
-grading by
setting
\deg\hbar=\deg\lambda=-2
.

Definition 5.2
.

A degree zero element
{\mathcal{V}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}[1]
of the form

{\mathcal{V}}=\sum_{g,n}{\mathcal{V}}_{g,n}\hbar^{g}\lambda^{2g-2+n},\;\;{\mathcal{V}}_{g,n}\in C_{6g-6+2n}(M^{\sf fr}_{g,0,n})_{\sf hS}

is called a
string vertex
if it satisfies the following properties:

1.

{\mathcal{V}}
is a Maurer-Cartan element in
{\mathfrak{g}}
; in components

\{{\mathcal{V}}_{g,n}\}
this means that we have

(\partial+uB){\mathcal{V}}_{g,n}+\Delta{\mathcal{V}}_{g-1,n+2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1}+g_{2}=g\\
n_{1}+n_{2}=n+2\end{subarray}}\{{\mathcal{V}}_{g_{1},n_{1}},{\mathcal{V}}_{g_{2},n_{2}}\}=0.

2.

{\mathcal{V}}_{0,3}=\frac{1}{6}\cdot{{\sf pt}}
with
{{\sf pt}}\in C_{0}(M^{\sf fr}_{0,0,3})_{\sf hS}
representing a point class.

A fundamental result about string vertices is the following theorem
from
[
Cos09
]
.

Theorem 5.3
.

A string vertex exists and is unique up to
homotopy (i.e., gauge equivalence between Maurer-Cartan elements).

Proof.

The proof is a standard argument in deformation theory. The
obstruction to existence lies in
H_{6g-7+2n}(M^{\sf fr}_{g,0,n})_{\sf hS}

while the deformation space is given by

H_{6g-6+2n}(M^{\sf fr}_{g,0,n})_{\sf hS}
. Both homology groups are known to
vanish. We refer to
[
Cos09
, Section 9]
for a detailed proof.
∎

5.3
The Koszul resolution of
{\mathfrak{g}}

We now describe the second DGLA
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
constructed from moduli spaces
of Riemann surfaces. Its construction is parallel to that of
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}

in (
4.2
).

For an integer
k\geq 1
denote by
\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}_{k}}[-k]
the rank
one local system over
M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr}
whose fiber over a Riemann
surface
(\Sigma,p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},q_{1},\ldots,q_{l},\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k},\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{l})

is the sign representation of the symmetric group
S_{k}
on the set
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\}
, shifted by
[-k]
. Denote by

p_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge p_{k}
its natural basis vector. For simplicity,
we often write this local system as
{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}
when the
integer
k
is clear from the context.

We shall construct a DGLA structure on the graded vector space

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}g,k,l\\
k\geq 1\end{subarray}}C_{*}\big(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{\sf hS}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt[2],

where the homotopy quotient is by
(S^{1})^{k+l}\ltimes S_{k}\times S_{l}

with the
S_{k}
action twisted by
{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}
. The homological
degree of an element

(\alpha,p_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge p_{k})\in C_{*}\big(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{\sf hS}
is explicitly denoted by

|\alpha|=\deg(\alpha)-k,

where
\deg(\alpha)
is the usual chain degree in the complex

C_{*}\big(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr})_{\sf hS}
, before the shifting by
[k]
operation.

The complex
C_{*}\big(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr})_{\sf hS}
has the equivariant
differential
\eth=\partial+uB
. After adding in the local system,
the complex
C_{*}\big(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{\sf hS}
has
differential

\eth(\alpha,p_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge p_{k})=(\eth\alpha,p_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge p_{k}).

The self-sewing operator

\Delta:C_{*}\big(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{\sf hS}\rightarrow C_{*}\big(M_{g+1,k,l-2}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{\sf hS}
is defined as
before, by

\Delta(\alpha,p_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge p_{k})=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq l}\Big(G_{ij}\big(\pi_{ij}(B_{i}\alpha)\big),p_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge p_{k}\Big)

where
G_{ij}:M^{\sf fr}_{g,k,l}\rightarrow M_{g+1,k,l-2}^{\sf fr}
is the self-sewing
map.

Define another operator
\iota:C_{*}\big(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{\sf hS}\rightarrow C_{*}\big(M_{g,k+1,l-1}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{\sf hS}
by setting

\iota\big(\alpha,p_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge p_{k}\big)=(-1)^{\deg(\alpha)}\sum_{j=1}^{l}\big(\iota_{j}\alpha,q_{j}\wedge p_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge p_{k}\big)

where
\iota_{j}:M^{\sf fr}_{g,k,l}\rightarrow M_{g,k+1,l-1}^{\sf fr}
is the map which
simply changes the output vertex
q_{j}
to become the first input
vertex and relabels the remaining vertices.

Lemma 5.4
.

We have
(\eth+\iota+\hbar\Delta)^{2}=0
.

Proof.

One verifies directly from the definitions that we have

[\eth,\iota]=[\iota,\iota]=[\iota,\Delta]=0.

∎

We proceed to define the Lie bracket of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
. It is a symmetric map

\{-,-\}_{\hbar}:\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}[1]\otimes\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}[1]\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}[1].

Recall that for a positive integer
r\geq 1
we have a map
G_{r}
that
sews the first
r
inputs of a surface in
M^{\sf fr}_{g^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}
with the
first
r
outputs of a surface in
M^{\sf fr}_{g^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime}}
. It induces a
map

G_{r}:C_{*}(M^{\sf fr}_{g^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}})\otimes C_{*}(M^{\sf fr}_{g^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime}})\rightarrow C_{*}(M^{\sf fr}_{g,k,l}),\mbox{ for }l^{\prime\prime}\geq r,\;k^{\prime}\geq r.

More generally, for a pair of multi-indices
J^{\prime}\subset K^{\prime}=\{1,\ldots,k^{\prime}\}
and
I^{\prime\prime}\subset L^{\prime\prime}=\{1,\ldots,l^{\prime\prime}\}
, both of size
r
,
there is a sewing map

G_{J^{\prime}I^{\prime\prime}}(\alpha,\beta)=G_{r}(\sigma_{J^{\prime},K^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}}\alpha,\sigma_{I^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime}\setminus I^{\prime\prime}}\beta),

where
\sigma_{J^{\prime},K^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}}
and
\sigma_{I^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime}\setminus I^{\prime\prime}}
are shuffle
permutations of
K^{\prime}
and
L^{\prime\prime}
respectively.

Define a twisted version

G^{B}_{r}:C_{*}(M^{\sf fr}_{g^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}})\otimes C_{*}(M^{\sf fr}_{g^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime}},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}})\rightarrow C_{*}(M^{\sf fr}_{g,k,l},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}),

by setting

\displaystyle G^{B}_{r}\big((\alpha,p^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge p^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}),(\beta,p^{\prime\prime}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge p^{\prime\prime}_{k^{\prime\prime}})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\deg(\beta)(k^{\prime}+r)}\big(G_{r}(\alpha,B_{q^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\cdots B_{q^{\prime\prime}_{r}}\beta),p^{\prime}_{r+1}\wedge\cdots p^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\wedge p^{\prime\prime}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge p^{\prime\prime}_{k^{\prime\prime}}\big).

Here
B_{q_{i}^{\prime\prime}}(\beta)
is the map on chains induced by the circle
operator rotating the framing of the
i
-th output of a Riemann
surface parametrized by
\beta
. The sign is chosen to match with the
algebraic construction of (
4.2
), see
Proposition
6.7
.

We can further extend this map to a map
\widetilde{G^{B}_{r}}
of
homotopy quotients by the circle actions, using the formula

\widetilde{G^{B}_{r}}(\Psi,\Phi)=G^{B}_{r}(\Psi\mid_{w_{1}^{-1}=\cdots=w_{r}^{-1}=0},\Phi\mid_{u_{1}^{-1}=\cdots=u_{r}^{-1}=0}).

Here the
w
’s are circle parameters at inputs while the
u
’s are
circle parameters at outputs. In other words we only apply the twisted sewing
map
G_{r}^{B}
when the circle parameters all have power zero at the
points to be sewed

Lemma 5.5
.

The twisted sewing map

\widetilde{G^{B}_{r}}:C_{*}\big(M_{g^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{(S^{1})^{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}}\otimes C_{*}\big(M_{g^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime}}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{(S^{1})^{k^{\prime\prime}+l^{\prime\prime}}}\rightarrow C_{*}\big(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{(S^{1})^{k+l}}

is a chain map.

Proof.

The twisted sewing map
\widetilde{G^{B}_{r}}
clearly
commutes with the geometric boundary map
\partial
. Since

\eth=\partial+uB
, it remains to prove its compatibility with the
circle actions. For circle parameters not involved in the sewing, this
is again clear. Let us consider the action by
uB_{q^{\prime\prime}_{1}}
. For an
equivariant chain
\Phi
we have

(u_{1}B_{q^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\Phi)|_{u_{1}^{-1}=0}\in\imag(B_{q^{\prime\prime}_{1}}).

This
shows that
\widetilde{G^{B}_{r}}(\Psi,uB_{q^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\Phi)=0
since

B_{q^{\prime\prime}_{1}}B_{q^{\prime\prime}_{1}}=0
.
∎

Symmetrizing along the
r
inputs and outputs yields our desired
composition map

\circ_{r}:C_{*}\big(M_{g^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{\sf hS}\otimes C_{*}\big(M_{g^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime}}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{\sf hS}\rightarrow C_{*}\big(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}\big)_{\sf hS}

explicitly given by

\Psi\circ_{r}\Phi=\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{\tau}\widetilde{G^{B}_{r}}(\sigma\Psi,\tau\Phi).

where the summation is over
\sigma\in{\sf Shuffle}(r,k^{\prime}-r)
and
\tau\in{\sf Shuffle}(r,l^{\prime\prime}-r)
which act by
permutations of
\{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}\}
and

\{q^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,q^{\prime\prime}_{l^{\prime\prime}}\}
respectively.

Theorem 5.6
.

Define an odd symmetric map
\{-,-\}_{\hbar}:\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}[1]\otimes\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}[1]\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}[1]
by

\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{\hbar}=(-1)^{|\Psi|}\sum_{r\geq 1}\big(\Psi\circ_{r}\Phi-(-1)^{|\Psi||\Phi|}\Phi\circ_{r}\Psi\big)\cdot\hbar^{r-1},

with the degrees
|\Psi|
,
|\Phi|
computed in
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
. The sign

(-1)^{|\Psi|}
is due to our convention to work with shifted signs
of DGLAs.

Then the triple

\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}},\eth+\iota+\hbar\Delta,\{-,-\}_{\hbar}\big)
forms a

\Z
-graded DGLA.

Proof.

This is analogous to the proof of Theorem
4.3
and
will be omitted.
∎

Proposition 5.7
.

There exists a quasi-isomorphism of DGLAs

\bar{\iota}:{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{g}},

where

{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}=\big(\bigoplus_{g,n\geq 1}C_{*}(M_{g,0,n}^{\sf fr})_{\sf hS}\big)\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt[1]

is endowed with the quotient DGLA structure of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in a matter
similar to that of
{\mathfrak{h}}
, see (
4.3
).

Proof.

Observe that for fixed
g\geq 0
and
n>0
the various
moduli spaces
M^{\sf fr}_{g,k,l}
with
k+l=n
are all isomorphic. Then
the result follows from the algebraic fact that if
W
is any

\Sigma_{n}
-representation, the associated Koszul complex

0\rightarrow W_{\Sigma_{n}}\rightarrow W_{\Sigma_{1,n-1}}\rightarrow W_{\Sigma_{2,n-2}}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{\Sigma_{n,0}}\rightarrow 0

is exact. Here
W_{\Sigma_{k,l}}
denotes the space of coinvariants
of
W
under the action of
\Sigma_{k}\times\Sigma_{l}
, where the
first group acts via the sign representation
{\mathsf{sgn}}_{k}
and the second
one acts via the trivial representation.
∎

5.4
Combinatorial string vertices

When
k\geq 1
and
l\geq 0
there exists a version of

C_{*}(M^{\sf fr}_{g,k,l})
constructed in terms of ribbon graphs with framed
inputs and outputs (also sometimes called fat graphs with black and
white vertices). This construction is due to
Kontsevich-Soibelman
[
KS09
]
, Costello
[
Cos07
]
and
Wahl-Westerland
[
WW16
]
. Denote this quasi-isomorphic chain
complex by
C_{*}^{\sf comb}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr})
; it is spanned by isomorphism
classes of framed and oriented ribbon graphs of genus
g
with
k

faces and
l
white vertices. We also have combinatorial versions
of the operators
\partial
,
B
,
\Delta
, and
\{-,-\}_{\hbar}
. Thus,
by the same construction of the previous subsection, we can define a
combinatorial version of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
, denoted by:

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{{\sf comb}}=\left(\bigoplus_{g,k\geq 1,l}C^{{\sf comb}}_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}})_{\sf hS}\right)\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt}[2].

It also forms a DGLA.

Theorem 5.8
.

There exists a roof diagram of quasi-isomorphisms of DGLAs:

{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{\iota}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}\longleftarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{{\sf comb}}.

Proof.

The left arrow is the morphism
\bar{\iota}
in
Theorem
5.6
. The right arrow is a direct
consequence of a result proved by Egas
[
Ega15
]
, that the
combinatorial sewing maps defined in
[
WW16
]
between ribbon
graphs agree with the geometric sewing maps.
∎

By the homotopy invariance of Maurer-Cartan moduli spaces,
Theorem
5.3
implies the following result.

Theorem 5.9
.

In the DGLA

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{{{\sf comb}}}=\left(\bigoplus_{g,k\geq 1,l}C^{{\sf comb}}_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}})\right)\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt

there exists a degree
-1
element

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}
, unique up to homotopy, of the form

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}=\sum_{g,k\geq 1,l}\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}_{g,k,l}\,\hbar^{g}\lambda^{2g-2+k+l},

such that the following conditions are
satisfied:

1.

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}
is a Maurer-Cartan element of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{{{\sf comb}}}
,
i.e.,
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}
satisfies the following equations for each triple

(g,k\geq 1,l)
:

(\partial+uB)\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}_{g,k,l}+\Delta\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}_{g-1,k,l+2}+\iota\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}_{g,k-1,l+1}+\frac{1}{2}\sum\frac{1}{r!}\{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}_{g_{1},k_{1},l_{1}},\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}_{g_{2},k_{2},l_{2}}\}_{r}=0.

The last sum is over all
r\geq 1
and all
(g_{1},g_{2},k_{1},k_{2},l_{1},l_{2})
such that

\displaystyle g_{1}+g_{2}+r-1

\displaystyle=g

\displaystyle k_{1}+k_{2}-r

\displaystyle=k

\displaystyle l_{1}+l_{2}-r

\displaystyle=l.

2.

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}_{0,1,2}=\frac{1}{2}\;\;\;\hbox to41.77pt{\vbox to21.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 20.88612pt\lower-2.10715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}} {}{{}}{}

corresponding to one-half times a point class in

C_{0}^{\sf comb}(M_{0,1,2}^{\sf fr})_{\sf hS}
.

Definition 5.10
.

We shall refer to
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}_{g,k,l}
as
combinatorial string
vertices
. Each of them is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear combination of ribbon
graphs.

Combinatorial string vertices can be explicitly computed by solving
increasingly large linear systems of equations: the Maurer-Cartan
equation determines
{\mathcal{V}}_{g,k,l}
uniquely (up to
(\partial+uB)
-exact
elements) from previously computed string vertices.

As examples, we present explicit formulas for the first few
combinatorial string vertices, ignoring orientations of ribbon graphs
and associated signs. We follow the conventions of
[
WW16
]
when
drawing ribbon graphs, except that we also add a cross at the end of
the unique leaf associated to an input cycle of the ribbon
graph. Since each string vertex is an equivariant chain, our
convention, with respect to the circle parameter, is that if we do not
indicate a power of
u
, then the corresponding face or output is
labeled by
u^{0}
.

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}_{0,1,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\;\;\;\hbox to41.77pt{\vbox to21.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 20.88612pt\lower-2.10715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}} {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Note, for example, that the coefficients
1/24
and
1/6
in

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1,0}^{\sf comb}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{0,1,3}^{\sf comb}
, respectively, can be
used to recover the integrals of a
\psi
-class on
{\overline{M}}_{1,1}
and

{\overline{M}}_{0,4}
, respectively. Indeed, we have

\int_{{\overline{M}}_{1,1}}\psi=\frac{1}{24},\;\;\;\int_{{\overline{M}}_{0,4}}\psi=3!\cdot\frac{1}{6}=1.

This is explained with detail in
[
CT17
]
.

Using computer calculations we are able to compute the string
vertices up to
{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}_{2,1,0}
. See
[
Cal24
]
for details on
the techniques used to speed up the computations.

6
The original, non-constructive definition of CEI

In this section we define the CEI potential
{\mathcal{D}}^{A,s}
of a cyclic

A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
and a splitting
s
. The definition we give in
this section is essentially the same as Costello’s
[
Cos09
]
, and
hence not yet constructive (it uses non-explicit inverses of certain
quasi-isomorphisms). We assume that the algebra
A
satisfies
condition
(\dagger)
in (
1.2
) and we continue to work
with the representation of the PROP of annuli

(L=C_{*}(A)[d],b,B,\langle\,-\,,\,-\,\rangle_{\sf Muk}).

6.1
Topological-conformal field theories

Our first goal is to construct a canonical Maurer-Cartan element

\widehat{\beta}^{A}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
. The dg-vector space
L
carries the structure of a 2d
TCFT, inducing a natural map of DGLAs

\rho^{A}:\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf comb}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}.

We take
\widehat{\beta}^{A}
to be the image of the string vertex

{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf comb}
under this map. See the discussion
in (
2.4
) for a conceptual guide to this construction.

Kontsevich-Soibelman
[
KS09
]
and Costello
[
Cos07
]
were the
first to sketch the construction of a 2d TCFT associated with a cyclic

A_{\infty}
-algebra. The state space of this TCFT is the complex

(L,b)
. Their construction was later treated in greater detail
(including signs) in Wahl-Westerland
[
WW16
]
; an improved
treatment of signs (making them explicitly computable) will appear
in
[
CC24-1
]
.

Theorem 6.1
.

The shifted reduced Hochschild chain complex
L=C_{*}(A)[d]
of a
cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
of Calabi-Yau dimension
d
carries
the structure of a positive boundary 2d TCFT. In other words there
are natural action maps for every
g\geq 0
,
k\geq 1
,
l\geq 0

satisfying
2-2g-k-l<0

\rho_{g,k,l}^{A}:C^{\sf comb}_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr})\rightarrow\Hom(L^{\otimes k},L^{\otimes l})[d(2-2g-2k)]

which are compatible with the sewing operations of framed Riemann
surfaces.

Remark 6.2
.

The signs involved in proving this theorem are pretty involved,
especially when
d
is odd. In
[
WW16
]
the authors work with
the usual Hochschild chain complex, without shifting by
d
. This
makes the treatment of signs rather not transparent for odd
d
.
Following Costello’s idea
[
Cos09
]
to include the shift
[d]

into the Hochschild chain complex, a more explicit and complete
description of the signs involved in the construction of
\rho^{A}

will be presented in the upcoming paper
[
CC24-1
]
.

Remark 6.3
.

The positive boundary condition is the requirement that
k\geq 1
in
the definition of the above maps. This is obvious from the fact
that ribbon graphs must have at least one face, but it is also
needed in order to avoid difficulties associated with the fact that

L
is in general infinite dimensional, so it cannot carry a
non-degenerate bilinear pairing.

Remark 6.4
.

Note that the shift by
d(2-2g-2k)
is even, so it will not affect
anything in the
\Z/2\Z
-graded cases. It will become relevant in
Appendix
A
.

Both
C^{\sf comb}_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr})
and
\Hom(L^{\otimes k},L^{\otimes l})
carry circle and symmetric group actions, so we can perform
the “
{\sf hS}
” operation on them – twisting the action by the local
system
{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}
on inputs, and taking homotopy quotients by the circle
actions and ordinary quotients by the symmetric group actions.

First, twisting the TCFT representation by
{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}
we get new maps

\displaystyle\rho_{g,k,l}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}:C^{\sf comb}_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Hom((L[1])^{\otimes k},L^{\otimes l})

\displaystyle\rho_{g,k,l}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}\big(\Gamma,p_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge p_{k}\big)

\displaystyle=\rho_{g,k,l}^{A}(\Gamma)\circ s^{\otimes k}

where
\Gamma
is an element of
C^{\sf comb}_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}})

and
s:L[1]\rightarrow L
is the shift map,

s(a)=(-1)^{|a|}a.

Recall that the differential of
L[1]
is
\partial=-b
, so that

\partial s=-bs
, and therefore
\rho_{g,k,l}^{A,{\sf tw}}
is a
chain map:

\displaystyle(\partial\rho_{g,k,l}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}})\big(\Gamma,p_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge p_{k}\big)

\displaystyle=b\rho_{g,k,l}^{A}(\Gamma)s^{\otimes k}-(-1)^{|\Gamma|}\rho_{g,k,l}^{A}(\Gamma)s^{\otimes k}(-b)

\displaystyle=b\rho_{g,k,l}^{A}(\Gamma)s^{\otimes k}-(-1)^{|\Gamma|+k}\rho_{g,k,l}^{A}(\Gamma)bs^{\otimes k}

\displaystyle=(\partial\rho_{g,k,l}^{A}(\Gamma))s^{\otimes k}

\displaystyle=\rho_{g,k,l}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}\big(\partial(\Gamma,p_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge p_{k})\big).

We remind the reader that the
{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}}
local system has a shift
[-k]

built into it, so the maps
\rho_{g,k,l}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}
respect grading.

Further taking homotopy quotient with respect to the action of

(S_{k}\times S_{l})\ltimes(S^{1})^{k+l}
we obtain action maps on

C^{\sf comb}_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}})_{\sf hS}
, which we will still denote
by
\rho_{g,k,l}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}
:

\rho_{g,k,l}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}:C^{\sf comb}_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}})_{\sf hS}\rightarrow\Hom^{\mathsf{c}}\big(\Sym^{k}(L_{+}[1]),\Sym^{l}L_{-}\big).

It is worth describing explicitly the graded vector space

C^{\sf comb}_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}})_{\sf hS}
. Consider the set of
oriented ribbon graphs of type
g,k,l
, whose input faces and output
white vertices have been marked with non-positive powers of
u
. An
isomorphism between such graphs is a ribbon graph isomorphism, allowed
to permute the input faces among themselves, as well as the output
white vertices among themselves, as long as the
u
-power markings are
matched. Such an isomorphism is said to be positively oriented if it
either preserves the orientation of the ribbon graphs and is an even
permutation on the faces, or gives the opposite orientation on the
ribbon graphs and is an odd permutation on the inputs. It will be
said to be negatively oriented otherwise. The space

C^{\sf comb}_{*}(M_{g,k,l}^{\sf fr},{\underline{{\mathsf{sgn}}}})_{\sf hS}
is the
\Q
-vector space
spanned by such
u
-marked graphs, modulo the relations

\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}
if there is a positively oriented isomorphism

\Gamma_{1}\cong\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma_{1}=-\Gamma_{2}
if there is a
negatively oriented one.

The degree of a
u
-marked graph whose inputs are labeled by

\{u^{-a_{i}}\}
and outputs by
\{u^{-b_{j}}\}
is

\deg(\Gamma,p_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge p_{k})=\deg(\Gamma)-k+2\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}+2\sum_{j=1}^{l}b_{j}.

The action of a
u
-marked graph
\Gamma
on
\Sym(L_{+}[1])
is done by
first matching
u
-powers, and then inserting the corresponding
elements of
L
into the inputs of the unmarked
\Gamma
with a sign
correction of
(-1)^{u\text{-power}}
at each input. The output

u
-powers multiply the elements of
L
read from the corresponding
outputs.

For example, in the case
k=1
,
l=1
, for a graph whose input is
labeled by
u^{-a}
and output by
u^{-b}
, the action is given
explicitly by

\rho_{g,1,1}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}\big(\Gamma\big)(\alpha\cdot u^{a^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}(-1)^{a+|\alpha|}\cdot\rho_{g,1,1}^{A}(\Gamma)(\alpha)\cdot u^{-b}&\mbox{ if }a=a^{\prime};\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(The sign
(-1)^{|\alpha|}
comes from the operator
s
, while

(-1)^{a}
is the sign correction above.) For general
k\geq 1
we need
to shuffle the inputs, while for
l\geq 1
we need to symmetrize the
outputs.

Lemma 6.5
.

The maps
\rho_{g,k,l}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}
are chain maps.

Proof.

We have already checked compatibility with the differential
b
, so
it only remains to prove the compatibility with
uB
. The operator

B
acts on a graph
\Gamma
by acting either on an input (call
this
B^{\sf in}
) or on an output (
B^{\sf out}
). Since the
twisting of the action does not affect the outputs in any way, the
compatibility with
B^{\sf out}
is obvious.

For the compatibility with
B^{\sf in}
we will again check the

k=1
,
l=1
case; the more general case of arbitrary
k
,
l
is
only slightly more complex in terms of notation. Assume that the
input of the graph is labeled by
u^{-a}
and the output by

u^{-b}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\rho_{g,1,1}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}\big(uB^{\sf in}(\Gamma)\big)(\alpha\cdot u^{a^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\delta_{a-1,a^{\prime}}\cdot(-1)^{a-1+|\alpha|}\cdot\rho_{g,1,1}^{A}(B^{\sf in}\Gamma)(\alpha)\cdot u^{-b}

\displaystyle=\delta_{a,a^{\prime}+1}\cdot(-1)^{a+|B\alpha|}\cdot\rho_{g,1,1}^{A}(\Gamma)(B\alpha)\cdot u^{-b}

\displaystyle=\rho_{g,1,1}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}(\Gamma)\left(uB(\alpha\cdot u^{a^{\prime}})\right).

∎

Remark 6.6
.

This calculation explains why the extra sign
(-1)^{u\text{-power}}

is needed in the definition of the action
\rho^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}
. This sign
also matches well with the sesquilinear property in the definition
of residue pairing, see (
3.2
). This sign is also
used in the following proposition to match the two
\iota
operators
in
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
, since
a priori
the
\iota
operator of

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
is
u
-linear, while the
\iota
operator of
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
is

u
-sesquilinear.

Proposition 6.7
.

The twisted TCFT structure maps
\{\rho_{g,k,l}^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}\}
induce
a morphism of DGLAs

\rho^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}:\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf comb}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}.

Proof.

This is evident from the construction, although one needs to check
the signs carefully. We omit the details.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

This proposition shows that the Sen-Zwiebach DGLA
{\mathfrak{g}}
(or,
equivalently,
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
) is the “universal” DGLA among DGLAs
constructed from TCFTs. This is more transparent than the point of
view in
[
CCT20
]
, where the DGLAs
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
were
constructed on equal footing.

6.2
Definition of
\widehat{\beta}^{A}

We now come to the main point of the first part of the construction of
CEI. In
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
we have a canonical
Maurer-Cartan element
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}
, the combinatorial string vertex

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}=\sum_{g,k\geq 1,l}\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{g,k,l}^{\sf comb}\,\hbar^{g}\,\lambda^{2g-2+k+l},

of Theorem
5.9
. Its push-forward under

\rho^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}
is then a canonical Maurer-Cartan element
\widehat{\beta}^{A}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
of the form

\widehat{\beta}^{A}=\rho^{A,{\sf tw}}_{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb})=\sum_{g,k\geq 1,l}\rho^{A,{\sf tw}}(\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{g,k,l}^{\sf comb})\,\hbar^{g}\,\lambda^{2g-2+k+l}.

The tensors

\widehat{\beta}^{A}_{g,k,l}=\rho^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}(\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\sf comb}_{g,k,l})\in\Hom^{\mathsf{c}}\big(\Sym^{k}(L_{+}[1]),\Sym^{l}L_{-}\big)

will play a key role in the definition and computation of the CEI of
the cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
. They only depend on
A
,
and not on any future choice of splitting.

6.3
Splittings of the non-commutative Hodge
filtration

To obtain invariants of
A
that are similar to those from
Gromov-Witten theory we need a further ingredient: a choice of
splitting
s
of the Hodge filtration.

Definition 6.9
.

A splitting of the non-commutative Hodge filtration of
A
is a
graded map of
\Z/2\Z
-graded vector spaces

s:H_{*}(L)\rightarrow H_{*}(L_{+})

satisfying the following two conditions:

S1.

(Splitting condition.)

s
splits the canonical
projection
H_{*}(L_{+})\rightarrow H_{*}(L)
.

S2.

(Lagrangian condition.)

\langle s(x),\,s(y)\rangle_{\sf hres}=\langle x,\,y\rangle_{\sf Muk}\mbox{ for any }x,y\in H_{*}(L)
.

Remark 6.10
.

In many circumstances it is useful to place further restrictions on
the allowed splittings. For example one may impose certain
homogeneity conditions, or ask for the splitting to be compatible
with the cyclic structure of the
A_{\infty}
-algebra. In this paper
we only need conditions S1 and S2, but we refer the reader
to
[
AT19
, Definition 3.7]
for more information on these
possible restrictions.

Since
A
is assumed to satisfy the Hodge-de Rham degeneration
property, we can endow the homology
H=H_{*}(L)
with the trivial
circle action. According to our conventions on circle actions we then
have the following graded vector spaces with trivial differentials:

H^{\mathsf{Tate}}=H\!\left(\hskip-2.33336pt\hbox{$\left(\hbox{$u$}\right)$}\hskip-2.33336pt\right)\!\hskip 0.66666pt,\;\;\;H_{+}=H\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$u$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt,\;\;\;H_{-}=H[u^{-1}].

A splitting
s:H\rightarrow H_{*}(L_{+})
, assumed to satisfy the
Lagrangian condition S2, extends by
u
-linearity
to an isomorphism of
symplectic
vector spaces.

s:\big(H^{\mathsf{Tate}},\langle-,-\rangle_{{\sf res}}\big)\rightarrow\big(H_{*}(L^{\mathsf{Tate}}),\langle-,-\rangle_{{\sf res}}\big).

6.4
Definition of CEI

Recall (Lemma
4.4
) that there is a quasi-isomorphism of
DGLAs

\bar{\iota}:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}.
