The Maurer-Cartan moduli space is invariant
2
2

2

We certainly need
to work with certain convergence properties when defining
Maurer-Cartan elements. In the current case, we require
Maurer-Cartan elements to have strictly positive power of

\lambda
.
under such quasi-isomorphisms. Thus there exists a
Maurer-Cartan element
\beta^{A}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}
,

\beta^{A}=\sum_{g,n\geq 1}\beta^{A}_{g,n}\,\hbar^{g}\,\lambda^{2g-2+n},

defined by the property that
\bar{\iota}(\beta^{A})
is gauge equivalent
to the element
\widehat{\beta}^{A}
defined above. By definition, it is unique up to
gauge equivalences.

Recall from (
4.3
) that we have a short exact
sequence of DGLAs

0\rightarrow\mathbb{K}[1]\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}\rightarrow 0.

The canonical vector space direct sum decomposition

{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}=\mathbb{K}[1]\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}

allows us to regard the Maurer-Cartan element
\beta^{A}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}
as an
element in
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
, by taking its

\mathbb{K}[1]\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt
-component to be zero
3
3

3

This
choice is only made for the sake of proving
Proposition
6.13
. A more reasonable choice would be to
force the dilaton equation. But this would present additional
difficulties in Proposition
6.13
.
. Even though

{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}
is not a sub-Lie algebra of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
, we will prove in
Lemma
7.2
that
\beta^{A}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
still satisfies the
Maurer-Cartan equation. We defer the proof of this fact until
Section
8
.

There is a well-known relationship between Maurer-Cartan elements of
the DGLA
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
with closed elements of the differential

b+uB+\hbar\Delta
, see Lemma
6.11
below. This
relationship involves computing the exponential
\exp(\beta/\hbar)

for a Maurer-Cartan element
\beta\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
. However, this
exponential does not make sense unless we impose additional finiteness
conditions. For this reason we extend the definition of the Weyl
algebra
{\mathcal{W}}\big(L^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)
to include
an additional even variable
\lambda
:

{\mathcal{W}}\big(L^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)=T\big(L^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt/\big(\alpha\otimes\beta-(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}\beta\otimes\alpha=\hbar\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\sf res}\big).\

We localize the Weyl algebra at
\hbar
and complete in the

\lambda
-adic topology to get the localized and completed algebra

\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}_{\hbar}\big(L^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)=\varprojlim_{n}{\mathcal{W}}\big(L^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)[\hbar^{-1}]/(\lambda^{n}).

Infinite power series
of the form

\sum_{k\geq 0}\alpha_{k}\lambda^{k}\hbar^{-k}

exist in

\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}_{\hbar}\big(L^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)
but not in

{\mathcal{W}}\big(L^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)
.

By analogy with the construction of
\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}(L^{\mathsf{Tate}})
we
denote by
\widehat{{\mathscr{F}}}_{A,\hbar}
and
\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}(L_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt
the localized and completed versions of the
Fock space and the symmetric algebra.

With these preparations we can state the following result, which is well
known.

Lemma 6.11
.

An element
\beta\in\lambda\cdot{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]
of even degree satisfies
the Maurer-Cartan equation

(b+uB+\hbar\Delta)\beta+\frac{1}{2}\{\beta,\beta\}=0

if and only if
{\mathcal{D}}=\exp(\beta/\hbar)
is

(b+uB+\hbar\Delta)
-closed in
\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}(L_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt
.

Moreover, two such Maurer-Cartan elements
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}

are gauge equivalent if and only if the corresponding elements

{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
are homologous. All these identities hold in
the algebra
\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}(L_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt
.

Using this lemma, the Maurer-Cartan element
\beta^{A}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
we
obtain a
(b+uB+\hbar\Delta)
-closed element

\exp(\beta^{A}/\hbar)\in\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}L_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt

in the
localized and completed symmetric algebra. The fact that the string
vertex is unique up to homotopy shows that the homology class of this
element depends only on the cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
. We denote
it by

\mbox{${\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{abs}}^{A}$}=\left[\mbox{$\exp$}(\beta^{A}/\hbar)\right]\in H_{*}\big(\mbox{$\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}$}L_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt,b+uB+\hbar\Delta\big)\cong H_{*}(\widehat{{\mathscr{F}}}_{\hbar})

and call it the
abstract total descendent potential
of
A
. It
is an even element in

H_{*}\big(\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}L_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt,b+uB+\hbar\Delta\big)
.

Note that the abstract potential
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{abs}}^{A}
is not yet of the form
we want; we’d like it to live in the homology of the same space, but
with respect to the
b
differential instead of
b+uB+\hbar\Delta
.
Our goal is therefore to use a splitting to construct an isomorphism

H_{*}\big(\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}L_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt,b+uB+\hbar\Delta\big)\cong H_{*}\big(\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}L_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt,b\big)=\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}H_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt.

In other words we want to homotopy trivialize the operators
B
and

\Delta
on the
b
-homology of
L
.

Given a splitting
s
of the non-commutative Hodge filtration of
A

we obtain an induced symplectomorphism

s:H_{*}(L)^{\mathsf{Tate}}\rightarrow H_{*}(L^{\mathsf{Tate}})
, which in turn induces an
isomorphism of Weyl algebras

\Phi^{s}:\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\hbar}\big(H_{*}(L)^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\hbar}\big(H_{*}(L^{\mathsf{Tate}})\big).

Define
\Psi^{s}
to be the composition of the three other maps in the
diagram below

\begin{CD}\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}_{\hbar}\big(H_{*}(L)^{{\sf Tate}}\big)@>{\Phi^{s}}>{}>\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}_{\hbar}\big(H_{*}(L^{{\sf Tate}})\big)=H_{*}\big(\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}_{\hbar}(L^{{\sf Tate}})\big)\\
@A{i}A{}A@V{}V{p}V\\
\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}H_{*}(L)_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt@>{\Psi^{s}}>{}>H_{*}\big(\widehat{\mbox{${\mathscr{F}}$}}_{\hbar}\big)\cong H_{*}(\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}L_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt)\end{CD}

It is a graded vector space isomorphism. Here the left vertical map
is the inclusion of the symmetric algebra generated by the Lagrangian
subspace
H_{*}(L)_{-}
into the Weyl algebra, while the right vertical
map is the canonical quotient map from the Weyl algebra to the Fock
space. The abstract total descendent potential
{\mathcal{D}}^{A}_{\mathsf{abs}}
lives in
the lower right corner.

Definition 6.12
.

The
total descendent potential

{\mathcal{D}}^{A,s}\in\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}H_{*}(L)_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt
of the pair
(A,s)
is the preimage of the
abstract total descendent potential
{\mathcal{D}}^{A}_{\mathsf{abs}}
under the map

\Psi^{s}
,

{\mathcal{D}}^{A,s}=(\Psi^{s})^{-1}({\mathcal{D}}^{A}_{\mathsf{abs}}).

The genus
g
,
n
-point function
F^{A,s}_{g,n}\in\Sym^{n}H_{*}(L)_{-}

is defined by the identity

\sum_{g,n}F^{A,s}_{g,n}\cdot\hbar^{g-1}\,\lambda^{2g-2+n}=\ln({\mathcal{D}}^{A,s}).

The right hand term
\ln({\mathcal{D}}^{A,s})
will be denoted by

F^{A,s}
.

6.5
Givental group action

Immediate from the above discussion is the compatibility of the
definition of the total descendent potential above with the action of
the Givental group on the space of splittings, which we discuss now.

Denote by
\mathfrak{G}_{A}
the Givental group of the pair

(H_{+},\langle\,-\,,\,-\,\rangle_{\sf Muk})
. Abstractly, it is the subgroup of
automorphisms of the symplectic vector space

(H^{\mathsf{Tate}},\langle\,-\,,\,-\,\rangle_{{\mathsf{res}}})
preserving the Lagrangian subspace
H_{+}

and acting as the identity on
H
. In explicit terms, an element of

\mathfrak{G}_{A}
is of the form

g=\id+g_{1}\cdot u+g_{2}\cdot u^{2}+\cdots

with each
g_{j}\in\End(H)
required to satisfy

\langle\,g\cdot x,\,g\cdot y\,\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}=\langle\,x,\,y\,\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}\quad\mbox{for any }x,y\in H^{\mathsf{Tate}}.

If the set of splittings of the non-commutative Hodge filtration is
nonempty, then it is a left torsor over the Givental group, by letting
an element
g\in\mathfrak{G}_{A}
act on a splitting

s:H_{+}\rightarrow u^{-1}H_{*}(L_{+})
by pre-composing with
g^{-1}
:

g\cdot s:H_{+}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g^{-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{+}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{*}(L_{+}).

An element
g
of the Givental group acts naturally on the Fock space

\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}H_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt
where the total descendent
potential lives, via the automorphism of the Weyl algebra induced from
the symplectic transformation
g
:

\begin{CD}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\hbar}\big(H^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)@>{\Phi^{g}}>{}>\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\hbar}\big(H^{\mathsf{Tate}}\big)\\
@A{i}A{}A@V{}V{\pi}V\\
\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}\,H_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt@>{\widehat{g}}>{}>\widehat{\Sym}_{\hbar}\,H_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt\end{CD}

Proposition 6.13
.

The construction of the total descendent potential is compatible
with the action of the Givental group
\mathfrak{G}_{A}
. Explicitly,
for a splitting
s
and an element
g\in\mathfrak{G}_{A}
we have

{\mathcal{D}}^{A,g\cdot s}=\widehat{g}({\mathcal{D}}^{A,s}).

Proof.

Consider the following diagram:

\begin{diagram}

We claim that

p\Phi^{g\cdot s}i=p\Phi^{s}i\pi\Phi^{g^{-1}}i.

The left hand side equals
p\Phi^{s}\Phi^{g^{-1}}i
. The composition

i\pi
in the middle is not the identity, but rather the projection
onto the negative subspace (image of
i
). Its kernel is by
definition the left ideal generated by the positive subspace

uH_{+}
. Thus we have
i\pi=\id\pmod{uH_{+}}
.

Now observe that
\Phi^{s}
preserves the ideal
uH_{+}
, just because by
definition a splitting
s
only contains non-negative powers in the
variable
u
. Followed by the projection map
p
gives zero, that is,

p\Phi^{s}i\pi\big(uH_{+}\big)=0.

We conclude that
p\Phi^{s}=p\Phi^{s}i\pi
, and hence

p\Phi^{g\cdot s}i=p\Phi^{s}\Phi^{g^{-1}}i=p\Phi^{s}i\pi\Phi^{g^{-1}}i.

This implies that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{A,g\cdot s}

\displaystyle=(p\Phi^{g\cdot s}i)^{-1}({\mathcal{D}}^{A}_{\mathsf{abs}})

\displaystyle=(\pi\Phi^{g^{-1}}i)^{-1}(p\Phi^{s}i)^{-1}({\mathcal{D}}^{A}_{\mathsf{abs}})

\displaystyle=(\pi\Phi^{g^{-1}}i)^{-1}({\mathcal{D}}^{A,s})=\widehat{g}({\mathcal{D}}^{A,s})

∎

7
The trivializing morphism
{\mathcal{K}}

In the previous section we constructed tensors
\widehat{\beta}^{A}_{g,k,l}
,
defined as the components of the image of the combinatorial string
vertex under the twisted TCFT map
\rho^{A,{\mathsf{tw}}}
. We want to
write explicit formulas expressing the CEI
F^{A,s}_{g,n}
in terms of
these tensors and a splitting
s
.

In order to accomplish this goal we shall prove that the DGLAs
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}

and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
are both “homotopically trivial” by constructing
explicitly computable
L_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphisms (that depend on a
splitting
s
) from them to simpler DGLAs
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
and

\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
, whose differentials only involve
b
,
B
and
\iota

(but not
\Delta
) and the bracket is trivial. The CEI potential is
then the push-forward of the abstract CEI potential defined earlier
under these trivializing quasi-isomorphisms.

This section is devoted to studying the comparison map

{\mathcal{K}}:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
; in the next one we shall construct the
corresponding quasi-isomorphism
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}
between the Koszul resolution
DGLA
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
and its trivialization
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
.

The DGLA structure on

{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}=\Sym(L_{-})[1]\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt

was constructed in (
4.1
) starting from the data of the
cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
. The differential of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
is

b+uB+\hbar\Delta
and the Lie bracket is
\{-,-\}
. Define another
DGLA
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
using the same underlying
\Z/2\Z
-graded vector
space, but endowed with differential
b+uB
and with trivial (zero)
Lie bracket. In this section we shall prove the following formality
result.

Theorem 7.1
.

A chain-level splitting
S:L\rightarrow L_{+}
of the non-commutative Hodge
filtration of
A
(Definition
7.3
) determines an

L_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphism

{\mathcal{K}}:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{{\mathsf{triv}}}.

An explicit formula for this quasi-isomorphism, in terms of
S
,
will be described below in (
7.3
).

As an immediate corollary we obtain the following result that was used
to define CEI (
6.4
).

Lemma 7.2
.

The element
\beta^{A}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}
of (
6.4
) is a
Maurer-Cartan element in
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
under the canonical inclusion of
dg-vector spaces
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}\subset{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
.

Proof.

From the explicit construction of
{\mathcal{K}}
(see below) it is clear
that
{\mathcal{K}}
restricts to an
L_{\infty}
-morphism

{\mathcal{K}}^{+}
which fits in the commutative
diagram below

\begin{CD}{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}@>{\pi}>{}>{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}@>{}>{}>0\\
@V{{\mathcal{K}}}V{}V@V{}V{{\mathcal{K}}^{+}}V\\
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}@>{\pi}>{}>({\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+})^{\mathsf{triv}}@>{}>{}>0.\end{CD}

Along the bottom row, since the Maurer-Cartan equation is simply

(b+uB)x=0
and the map
\pi
is surjective at chain level, it is
obvious that the Maurer-Cartan element
{\mathcal{K}}^{+}(\beta^{A})
can be
lifted to
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
. The two vertical maps are both
L_{\infty}

quasi-isomorphisms, therefore there exists a Maurer-Cartan element

\alpha\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
such that
\pi(\alpha)
is gauge equivalent to

\beta^{A}
, i.e., there exists a degree one element
g\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}
for
which
\exp(g)\cdot\pi(\alpha)=\beta^{A}
. Since
\pi
is surjective,
let
\widetilde{g}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
be a lift of
g
in
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
. Then the
Maurer-Cartan element
\exp(\widetilde{g})\cdot\alpha
lifts the
Maurer-cartan element
\beta^{A}
. Modifying such an element in its
scalar part does not affect the property of it being Maurer-Cartan
(as
\mathbb{K}[1]\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt
is central in
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
), so we can
choose the lift to have trivial scalar part.
∎

7.1
Chain-level splittings and their inverses

Definition 7.3
.

A chain-level splitting of the Hodge filtration is a map of
complexes
S:L\rightarrow L_{+}
of the form
S=\id+S_{1}u+S_{2}u^{2}+\cdots
with each
S_{n}\in\End(L)
. A chain-level splitting
S

is called symplectic if it satisfies

\langle S\alpha,S\beta\rangle_{\sf hres}=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\sf Muk}

for any
\alpha,\beta\in L
. It is called homologically symplectic
if the above equation holds for
\alpha,\beta\in\ker b
, i.e. the
induced map
S:H_{*}(L)\rightarrow H_{*}(L_{+})
satisfies the symplectic condition.

In explicit terms the symplectic condition means that the coefficients
of all higher powers of
u
in the higher residue pairing above
vanish, i.e.,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N}(-1)^{j}\langle S_{j}\alpha,S_{N-j}\beta\rangle_{\sf Muk}=0,\;\forall N\geq 1,\;\forall\alpha,\beta\in L.

A chain-level splitting
S:L\rightarrow L_{+}
can be extended by

u
-linearity to an isomorphism of chain complexes

\big(L\!\left(\hskip-2.33336pt\hbox{$\left(\hbox{$u$}\right)$}\hskip-2.33336pt\right)\!\hskip 0.66666pt,b\big)\rightarrow L^{\sf Tate}
which we shall still
denote by
S
. Both sides carry residue pairings, and the
symplectic condition above is equivalent to saying that
S
is a
symplectomorphism. Indeed, for any
x,y\in L\!\left(\hskip-2.33336pt\hbox{$\left(\hbox{$u$}\right)$}\hskip-2.33336pt\right)\!\hskip 0.66666pt
we have

\displaystyle\langle Sx,Sy\rangle_{{\mathsf{res}}}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ the
coefficient of $u$ in }\langle Sx,Sy\rangle_{{\mathsf{hres}}}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ the coefficient of $u$ in }\langle x,y\rangle_{{\mathsf{hres}}}

\displaystyle=\langle x,y\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}

The following lemma proves that any splitting
s:H_{*}(L)\rightarrow H_{*}(L_{+})

at homology level, in the sense of Definition
6.9
,
lifts to a homologically symplectic chain-level splitting

S:L\rightarrow L_{+}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
s:H_{*}(L)\rightarrow H_{*}(L_{+})
be a splitting of the Hodge
filtration. Then there exists a chain-level splitting
S:L\rightarrow L_{+}

that lifts the map
s
. In particular, since
s
is always assumed by
definition to be symplectic, its lift
S
will be homologically symplectic.

Proof.

We shall construct a chain-level lift
S
of
s
, order by order in
the powers of
u
. As a first step we need to find
S_{1}\in\End(L)

satisfying
[b,S_{1}]=-B
. Since we work over a field there exist
maps of complexes

i:H_{*}(L)\rightarrow L\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;p:L\rightarrow H_{*}(L)

such that
pi=\id
. This induces a quasi-isomorphism

I:\End^{*}(H_{*}(L))\rightarrow\End^{*}(L),\;\;\varphi\mapsto i\varphi p.

The map
B
induces the zero map on
H_{*}(L,b)
, because we assume
that the Hodge-de Rham spectral sequence degenerates at
{}^{1}E
.
Therefore
B
induces an exact operator on
\End^{*}(L)
, so we
can choose an endomorphism
S_{1}^{\prime}
of
L
so that
[b,S_{1}^{\prime}]=B
.
Now we can modify
S_{1}^{\prime}
by an element in the image
\imag(I)
to
obtain an endomorphism
S_{1}
so that
\id+uS_{1}
lifts the desired
splitting map
s
, modulo
(u^{2})
.

Now assume by induction that we have already found a lift

\id+uS_{1}+\cdots+u^{n-1}S_{n-1}
of
s
, modulo
(u^{n})
. We are
looking for
S_{n}\in\End^{*}(L)
satisfying

[b,S_{n}]=-BS_{n-1}.

By construction we have

[b,BS_{n-1}]=-B[b,S_{n-1}]=BBS_{n-2}=0.

Hence
BS_{n-1}
induces a map in homology
\End^{*}(H_{*}(L))
. Observe
that this map is exactly the
n
-th page map of the spectral
sequence associated to the
u
-filtration on
L_{+}
, and hence again
this map vanishes by the Hodge-to-de-Rham degeneration
property. Thus we can find
S_{n}^{\prime}
satisfying

[b,S_{n}^{\prime}]=-BS_{n-1}
and we can modify it by elements in the image

\imag(I)
to obtain the desired endomorphism
S_{n}
.
∎

Let
S
be a chain-level splitting of the Hodge filtration, extended
by linearity to a quasi-isomorphism

S:\big(L\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$u$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt,b\big)\rightarrow\big(L\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$u$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt,b+uB\big).

Since the
u^{0}
component of
S
is the identity we can write the
inverse
R
of
S
explicitly, in the form

R=\id+R_{1}u+R_{2}u^{2}+\cdots,

where the
R_{i}
’s are endomorphisms of the graded vector space
L
.
More precisely, we enforce the sequence of identities

\sum_{i+j=k}R_{i}S_{j}=\begin{cases}\id&\mbox{ if }k=0\\
0&\mbox{ if }k\geq 1\end{cases}.

These can be recursively solved to get formulas for the operators

R_{j}
’s in terms of the
S_{i}
’s. For example we have

R_{1}=-S_{1},\;\;R_{2}=-S_{2}+S_{1}S_{1},\;\;\mbox{etc.}

This gives the desired inverse chain map

R:\big(L\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$u$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt,b+uB\big)\rightarrow\big(L\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$u$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt,b\big).

Since
S
and
R
are chain maps, we have

[b,S_{n}]=-BS_{n-1},\;\;[b,R_{n}]=R_{n-1}B\;\;\mbox{for
all}\;\;n\geq 1.

7.2
A first order trivialization

Recall that, by construction, the restriction of the operator

\Delta:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
to
\Sym^{2}L_{-}
is given by

\Omega(x,y)=\langle Bx_{0},y_{0}\rangle_{\sf Muk},

see (
4.1
). Having a chain-level splitting
S
of the
Hodge filtration (along with its inverse
R
) allows us to
homotopy-trivialize
\Omega
, which in turn will allow us to
homotopy-trivialize
\Delta
. This is the first step in constructing
the quasi-isomorphism
{\mathcal{K}}
.

Using
S
and
R
we can define an even map

H:L_{-}\otimes L_{-}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}

which will be a homotopy trivialization of
\Omega
. It is the
defined by takings its components

H_{i,j}:u^{-i}L\otimes u^{-j}L\rightarrow\mathbb{K}

to be, for
i,j\geq 0
, given by

H_{i,j}(u^{-i}x,u^{-j}y)=\langle\,(-1)^{j}\sum_{l=0}^{j}S_{l}R_{i+j+1-l}\,x,\,y\,\rangle.

Equivalently, we can define the map
H
more compactly by the formula

H(\alpha,\beta)=-\langle S\,\tau_{\geq 1}\,R(\alpha),\beta\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}

for
\alpha,\beta\in L_{-}
. Here
\tau_{\geq 1}:L^{\mathsf{Tate}}\rightarrow u\cdot L_{+}
is the projection onto the part with positive powers of
u
.

Proposition 7.5
.

For any
\alpha,\beta\in L_{-}
we have

H\big((b+uB)\alpha,\beta\big)+(-1)^{|\alpha|}H\big(\alpha,(b+uB)\beta\big)=-\Omega(\alpha,\beta).

In other words, the operator
H
is a bounding homotopy of
\Omega
:

[b+uB,H]=\Omega
.

Proof.

The proof is a direct calculation using the explicit formula for
H

and properties of
S
and
R
.
∎

Having constructed
H
we can now define a first order trivialization
of
\Delta
as follows. Symmetrize the homotopy operator
H
to
obtain an operator
H^{{\sf sym}}:\Sym^{2}L_{-}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}
,

H^{{\sf sym}}(x\cdot y)=\frac{1}{2}\left(H(x,y)+(-1)^{|x||y|}H(y,x)\right).

Since
\Omega
is by definition symmetric (
3.2
)
we conclude that

[b+uB,H^{{\sf sym}}]=\Omega.

Extending
H^{\sf sym}
as a second order differential operator to the full
symmetric algebra
\Sym(L_{-})
yields a new operator which we denote
by
\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}
:

\Delta^{H^{{\sf sym}}}:\Sym(L_{-})\rightarrow\Sym(L_{-}).

It is now obvious that
\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}
homotopy-trivializes

\Delta
: both sides are second order differential operators, thus it
suffices to prove that they are equal on
\Sym^{\leq 2}L_{-}
, in which
case this is exactly the equation
[b+uB,H^{{\sf sym}}]=\Omega
.

For the purposes of this section any chain-level splitting
S
(not
necessarily homologically symplectic) works well. However, this is not
the case in the next section where the symmetric property of
H
is
essential. In order to deal with this problem, we prove two additional
lemmas that will be used later.

Lemma 7.6
.

If
S
is a symplectic chain-level splitting, then the homotopy
operator
H
associated with a chain-level splitting
S
is
symmetric, i.e.
H=H^{\sf sym}
.

Proof.

This is a direct calculation using the definitions.
∎

In general we do not know if a chain-level symplectic splitting exists
lifting a given splitting on homology. However, the following lemma
shows that even if we use a non-symplectic lift, still the homotopy
operator
H
is always symmetric up to homotopy (if the splitting is
homologically symplectic).

Lemma 7.7
.

Assume that
S
is a homologically symplectic chain-level
splitting. Then there exists an odd operator
\delta:L_{-}\otimes L_{-}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}
such that

[b+uB,\delta]=H-H^{\sf sym}.

Proof.

Denote by
\sigma:L_{-}\otimes L_{-}\rightarrow L_{-}\otimes L_{-}
the
transposition operator
x\otimes y\mapsto(-1)^{|x||y|}y\otimes x
. Since
H^{\sf sym}=\frac{1}{2}(H+H\sigma)
, it suffices to show that

D=H-H\sigma
is
(b+uB)
-exact. First we show it is
b+uB
-closed:

[b+uB,D]=[b+uB,H]-[b+uB,H]\sigma=\Omega-\Omega\sigma=0.

Thus,
D
descends to a map in homology

H_{*}(L_{-})\otimes H_{*}(L_{-})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}
. We shall prove that this map is
zero. There is an increasing filtration of
L_{-}
defined by

F_{k}L_{-}=\{x\in L_{-}\mid x\mbox{ is of the form\;\;}x_{-k}u^{-k}+\cdots+x_{0}u^{0}.\}

It induces an exhaustive filtration in homology

H_{*}(L_{-})=\lim H_{*}(F_{k}L_{-}).

We shall prove that
D
is zero in homology by induction on
k=i+j

in the tensor product filtration
H_{*}(F_{i}L_{-})\otimes H_{*}(F_{j}L_{-})
.

To begin, consider the case
x=x_{0}\in F_{0}L_{-}
and

y=y_{0}\in F_{0}L_{-}
. The
(b+uB)
-closedness of
x
and
y
gives

bx=by=0
. We have

D(x,y)=\langle R_{1}x,y\rangle_{\sf Muk}-\langle x,R_{1}y\rangle_{\sf Muk}

Since
R_{1}=-S_{1}
, the above is indeed zero by the homologically
symplectic condition, see Definition
7.3
.

For the general case let
x=x_{-i}u^{-i}+\cdots
and

y=y_{-j}u^{-j}+\cdots
be two elements in
L_{-}
that are

(b+uB)
-closed. In particular, we have
bx_{-i}=by_{-j}=0
. We
rewrite
x
and
y
as

x=\tau_{\leq 0}S(x_{-i}u^{-i})+x^{\prime},\;\;\;y=\tau_{\leq 0}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})+y^{\prime},

with
x^{\prime}\in F_{i-1}L_{-}
,
y^{\prime}\in F_{j-1}L_{-}
and both

(b+uB)
-closed. By induction, it suffices to prove that

D\big(\tau_{\leq 0}S(x_{-i}u^{-i}),\tau_{\leq 0}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\big)=0.

We first compute

\displaystyle H\big(\tau_{\leq 0}S(x_{-i}u^{-i}),

\displaystyle\tau_{\leq 0}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\big)=H\big(S(x_{-i}u^{-i})-\tau_{\geq 1}S(x_{-i}u^{-i}),\tau_{\leq 0}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\big)

\displaystyle=-\langle S\tau_{\geq 1}R\big(S(x_{-i}u^{-i})-\tau_{\geq 1}S(x_{-i}u^{-i})\big),\tau_{\leq 0}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}.

Observe that we have

\displaystyle S\tau_{\geq 1}RS(x_{-i}u^{-i})

\displaystyle=S\tau_{\geq 1}(x_{-i}u^{-i})=0,

\displaystyle S\tau_{\geq 1}R\tau_{\geq 1}S(x_{-i}u^{-i})

\displaystyle=\tau_{\geq 1}S(x_{-i}u^{-i}).

The earlier expression simplifies to

H\big(\tau_{\leq 0}S(x_{-i}u^{-i}),\tau_{\leq 0}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\big)=\langle\tau_{\geq 1}S(x_{-i}u^{-i}),\tau_{\leq 0}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}.

Similarly, for the term
H\sigma
we have

H\sigma\big(\tau_{\leq 0}S(x_{-i}u^{-i}),\tau_{\leq 0}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\big)=-\langle\tau_{\leq 0}S(x_{-i}u^{-i}),\tau_{\geq 1}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}.

Taking the difference yields

\displaystyle D\big(\tau_{\leq 0}

\displaystyle S(x_{-i}u^{-i}),\tau_{\leq 0}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\big)=

\displaystyle=\langle\tau_{\geq 1}S(x_{-i}u^{-i}),\tau_{\leq 0}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}+\langle\tau_{\leq 0}S(x_{-i}u^{-i}),\tau_{\geq 1}S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}

\displaystyle=\langle S(x_{-i}u^{-i}),S(y_{-j}u^{-j})\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}=\langle x_{-i}u^{-i},y_{-j}u^{-j}\rangle_{\mathsf{res}}=0.

In the third equality we have used the homologically symplectic condition
which is valid since both
x_{-i}
and
y_{-j}
both
b
-closed.
∎

7.3
Construction of the

L_{\infty}
morphism
{\mathcal{K}}

The idea for proving Theorem
7.1
is as follows. First we
construct a pseudo-isotopic family of DGLAs which interpolates between

{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
. By definition, this is a DGLA structure on
the tensor product
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\otimes\Omega_{[0,1]}^{\bullet}
which
specializes to
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
at
t=0
and to
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
at
t=1
. Then we
apply an integration procedure to obtain the desired
L_{\infty}

isomorphism
{\mathcal{K}}:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
.

Using the homotopy operator
H^{\sf sym}
defined above we define a DGLA
structure on
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\otimes\Omega_{[0,1]}^{\bullet}
as follows. We take
the differential to be

b+uB+\hbar t\Delta+d_{DR}+\hbar\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}dt,

with
\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}
defined as in (
7.2
), and we
take the Lie bracket defined by

t\cdot\{-,-\}+\{-,-\}^{H^{\sf sym}}dt,

with
\{-,-\}^{H^{\sf sym}}
defined as the failure of
\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}

to be a first order differential operator on
\Sym{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
. This is
similar to how
\{-,-\}
was defined in (
4.1
).

Lemma 7.8
.

With the differential and Lie bracket defined above

{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\otimes\Omega_{[0,1]}^{\bullet}
is a DGLA.

Proof.

This is a direct check using
Proposition
7.5
.
∎

As described in
[
Fuk10
]
one can integrate this family of DGLAs to
obtain explicit
L_{\infty}
isomorphisms

{\mathcal{K}}(t):{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}(t)\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}(0)={\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}.

In particular, for
t=1
we obtain the desired isomorphism

{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}(1)
. The formulas for
K
in
[
Fuk10
]
requires the
computation of integrals on certain configuration spaces. We shall not
go into the details of these calculations but instead, in the
remainder of this section, we shall write down the final result which
turns out to be quite neat. The
L_{\infty}
morphism
{\mathcal{K}}
is given by
a Feynman sum over a certain class of graphs, with the vertices decorated
by the tensors
\beta^{A}_{g,n}
from (
6.4
), and with the
edges decorated by the operator
H^{\sf sym}
defined earlier in terms of
the splitting
s
!

Before we give the exact formula for
{\mathcal{K}}
we need to introduce some
notations for graphs, following
[
GK98
, Section 2]
. By a labeled
graph we shall mean a graph
G
(possibly with leaves) endowed with a
genus labeling function
g:V_{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
on the set of its
vertices
V_{G}
. The genus of a labeled graph is defined to be

g(G)=\sum_{v\in V_{G}}g(v)+{\sf rank\,}H_{1}(G).

We will use the following notations for a labeled graph
G
:

–

V_{G}
denotes the set of vertices of
G
;

–

L_{G}
denotes the set of leaves of
G
;

–

The valency of a vertex
v\in V_{G}
is denoted by
n(v)
;

–

The graph
G
is called stable if
2g(v)-2+n(v)>0
for
every vertex
v
.

–

If
G
has
m
vertices, a marking of
G
is a bijection

f:\{1,\cdots,m\}\rightarrow V_{G}.

An isomorphism between two marked
and labeled graphs is an isomorphism of the underlying labeled graphs
that also preserves the markings.

We will denote various isomorphism classes of graphs as follows:

–

\Gamma(g,n)
is the set of isomorphism classes of
graphs of genus
g
with
n
leaves;

–

Using double brackets as in
\Gamma\!\left(\hskip-2.33336pt\hbox{$\left(\hbox{$g,n$}\right)$}\hskip-2.33336pt\right)\!\hskip 0.66666pt
requires
further that the graphs be stable;

–

A subscript
m
as in
\Gamma(g,n)_{m}
or

\Gamma\!\left(\hskip-2.33336pt\hbox{$\left(\hbox{$g,n$}\right)$}\hskip-2.33336pt\right)\!\hskip 0.66666pt_{m}
indicates that the graphs in discussion have

m
vertices;

–

adding a tilde as in
\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m}
or

\widetilde{\Gamma\!\left(\hskip-2.33336pt\hbox{$\left(\hbox{$g,n$}\right)$}\hskip-2.33336pt\right)\!\hskip 0.66666pt}_{m}
means that we consider

marked
graphs.

We are now ready to write down the formula for the desired
L_{\infty}

morphism
{\mathcal{K}}:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
. Recall that an

L_{\infty}
morphism is a collection of even linear maps

{\mathcal{K}}_{m}:\Sym^{m}({\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1])\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}[1]

defined for each
m\geq 1
. Since
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}=\Sym(L_{-})[1]\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt
already includes a shift
by one, the parity of the elements in
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]
is the same as in

\Sym L_{-}
.

The
m
-th component
{\mathcal{K}}_{m}
of
{\mathcal{K}}
will then be the

\lambda
-linear extension of the map

{\mathcal{K}}_{m}=\sum_{g,n}\sum_{(G,f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(G,f)|}\cdot{\mathcal{K}}_{(G,f)},

with the map
{\mathcal{K}}_{(G,f)}
described below.

Let
(G,f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m}
be a marked graph. Set

g_{i}=g(f(i))
, and
n_{i}=n(f(i))
. We proceed to describe the
construction of the map

{\mathcal{K}}_{(G,f)}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\left(\Sym^{n_{i}}L_{-}\cdot\hbar^{g_{i}}\right)\rightarrow\Sym^{n}L_{-}\cdot\hbar^{g}.

For elements
\gamma_{i}\in\Sym^{n_{i}}L_{-},\;(i=1,\ldots,m)
the
expression

{\mathcal{K}}_{(G,f)}(\gamma_{1}\hbar^{g_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\hbar^{g_{m}})
is
computed by the following Feynman-type procedure:

1.

For an element
\gamma=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{j}\in\Sym^{j}L_{-}
let

\widetilde{\gamma}\in\left(L_{-}\right)^{\otimes j}
be its
desymmetrization,

\widetilde{\gamma}=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{j}}\epsilon\cdot x_{\sigma(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{\sigma(j)}\in L_{-}^{\otimes j}.

Here
\epsilon
is the Koszul sign for permuting the elements

x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}
(with respect to the degree in
L_{-}
). Then
decorate the half-edges adjacent to each vertex
v_{i}
by

\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}
. Tensoring together the results over all the
vertices yields a tensor of the form

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma_{i}}\in L_{-}^{\otimes(\sum n_{i})}.

The order in which these elements are tensored is the one
given by the marking.

2.

For each internal edge
e
of
G
contract the corresponding
components of the above tensor using the even symmetric bilinear form

H^{{\sf sym}}:L_{-}^{\otimes 2}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}.

When applying the
contraction we always permute the tensors to bring the two terms
corresponding to the two half-edges to the front, and then apply the
contraction map. The ordering of the set
E_{G}
does not matter since
the operator
H^{{\sf sym}}
is even; also the ordering of the two
half-edges of each edge does not matter since the operator
H^{{\sf sym}}

is (graded) symmetric.

3.

Read off the remaining tensor components (corresponding to the
leaves of the graph) in any order, and regard the result as the
element
\gamma
in
\Sym^{n}L_{-}
via the canonical projection map

L_{-}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\Sym^{n}L_{-}
.

To illustrate the construction, we write down the map
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
. The
following figures illustrate a few graphs that contribute in this
case. Their common feature is that they have only one vertex in which
case the marking
f
is unique.

The contribution of the first type of graphs (the star
graphs) is the identity map

{\mathfrak{h}}[1]=\Sym L_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{triv}}=\Sym L_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt.

To see this, note that
de-symmetrization followed by projection produces a factor of
n!
,
which is canceled by the size of the automorphism group of the star
graph. We also note that it is necessary to use the set
\Gamma(g,n)

of all labeled graphs, as opposed to using just stable graphs. For
instance, the
(g,n)=(0,0)
star graph (just a vertex with a genus
label of
0
) contributes to the identity map
\Sym^{0}L_{-}=\mathbb{K}\rightarrow\Sym^{0}L_{-}=\mathbb{K}
.

In a similar way one checks that the middle type of graph acts
precisely by the operator
\hbar\Delta^{H^{{\sf sym}}}
, while the third
type of graph contributes

\frac{1}{2}(\hbar\Delta^{H^{{\sf sym}}})^{2}
. Here the extra factor
1/2

comes from the automorphism that exchanges the two loops. In general
we have

{\mathcal{K}}_{1}=\sum_{l\geq 0}\frac{1}{l!}(\hbar\Delta^{H^{{\sf sym}}})^{l}=e^{\hbar\Delta^{H^{{\sf sym}}}}.

Indeed, the first
L_{\infty}
morphism
identity is
(b+uB){\mathcal{K}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}_{1}(b+uB+\hbar\Delta)
. It can be verified
directly using the formula above of
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and
Proposition
7.5
.

7.4
Proof of the fact that
{\mathcal{K}}
is an

L_{\infty}
morphism

The remainder of this section is devoted to proving
Theorem
7.1
. Replacing the contraction operator
H^{{\sf sym}}
by

tH^{{\sf sym}}
in the construction of
{\mathcal{K}}
yields a family of
pre-
L_{\infty}
morphisms

{\mathcal{K}}(t):{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}(t)\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}(0)={\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}.

On the other hand, the integration procedure described in
[
Fuk10
]

produces a family of
L_{\infty}
morphisms denoted by

{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}(t):{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}(t)\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}(0)={\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}.

It will suffice to prove
{\mathcal{K}}(t)={\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}(t)
. To accomplish
this it is most convenient to work with the bar construction of
DGLAs. Indeed, recall that we have defined the operator

\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]

which is a first order trivialization of
\Delta
,
i.e.
[b+uB,\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}]=\Delta
. Taking the commutator with the
product of the algebra
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]=\Sym L_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda]$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt
yields the
binary operation

\{x,y\}^{H^{{\sf sym}}}:=\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}(x\cdot y)-\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}(x)\cdot y-x\cdot\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}(y).

Using the bar construction of DGLAs we extend the even maps

\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}
and
\{-,-\}^{H^{{\sf sym}}}
to coderivations

\nu_{1},\nu_{2}:\Sym({\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1])\rightarrow\Sym({\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]).

Then, by the construction in
[
Fuk10
, Section 9]
, the family

{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}(t)
is characterized by the differential equation

\frac{d{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{m}(t)}{dt}=\hbar{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}(t)_{m}\nu_{1}+{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2},

with the initial condition that
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}(0)=\id
.

We proceed to prove that our family
{\mathcal{K}}(t)
also satisfies
the above differential equation and initial condition, which will show
that the two morphisms are equal. Recall that by definition we have

{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(t)=\sum_{g,n}\sum_{(G,f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(G,f)|}\cdot{\mathcal{K}}_{(G,f)}(t)\cdot\hbar^{g-\sum_{i=1}^{m}g(f(i))}.

The dependence of
{\mathcal{K}}^{S}_{(G,f)}(t)
on
t
is simple: we
assign
tH^{{\sf sym}}
to all edges of
G
. Thus, we observe that when
the differentiation acts on loop edges it yields the term

\hbar{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}(t)_{m}\nu_{1}
, while differentiating at non-loop
edges yields the other term
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}
.

In both cases the relevant coefficients involving automorphism group
of graphs are standard in the theory of graph sums. For example we
can use the techniques of integration over groupoids developed
in
[
Fio06
]
to argue their validity as follows. Let us denote by

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
the groupoid whose objects are marked labeled graphs
(G,f)

with
m
vertices, and morphisms are graph isomorphisms preserving
marking and labelings. Let
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{m}
denote the groupoid whose objects
are triples
(G,f,e)
with
e
a non-loop edge of
G
. We have the
following maps of groupoids

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf F}}}{{\longleftarrow}}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf C}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{D}}_{m-1}.

The functor
{\sf F}
is by forgetting the edge
e
, and the functor

{\sf C}
is
by contracting the edge
e
. The new vertex obtained from contracting

e
in the contracted graph
G/e
is marked by
1
, while the
remaining vertices of
G/e
are marked according to its ordering
defined by
f
. As in
[
Fio06
]
we may consider the assignment

(G,f)\mapsto{\mathcal{K}}_{(G,f)}(t)\cdot\hbar^{g-\sum_{i=1}^{m}g(f(i))}

as a vector-valued function
{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(t)
on the groupoid

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
, and the coefficient
\frac{1}{|\Aut(G,f)|}
as a measure

d\mu
on the set of isomorphism classes of objects. In this way we
can rewrite the summation as

\sum_{g,n}\sum_{(G,f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(G,f)|}\cdot{\mathcal{K}}_{(G,f)}(t)\cdot\hbar^{g-\sum_{i=1}^{m}g(f(i))}=\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{m}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(t)d\mu

The
t
-dependence is a
t
factor for every edge of
G
, which
implies that we simply have a factor
t^{|E_{G}|}
, and therefore we get

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{m}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(t)d\mu=\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{m}}|E_{G}|t^{|E_{G}|-1}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}d\mu.

Using the basic properties of groupoid integrals we
can also simplify the right hand side of the differential equation
satisfied by
{\mathcal{K}}_{m}^{\prime}(t)
as

\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{m-1}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}d\mu=\int_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{m}}{\sf C}^{*}\big({\mathcal{K}}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}\big)d\mu=\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{m}}{\sf F}_{*}{\sf C}^{*}\big({\mathcal{K}}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}\big)d\mu,

where the first and second equalities follow from
[
Fio06
, Equation
6.1]
and
[
Fio06
, Proposition 3.2]
, respectively.

The vector-valued function

{\sf F}_{*}{\sf C}^{*}\big({\mathcal{K}}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}\big)
is computed as

\displaystyle{\sf F}_{*}{\sf C}^{*}\big({\mathcal{K}}_{m-1}(t)

\displaystyle\nu_{2}\big)(G,f)=\sum_{e\in E_{G}^{{\sf non-loop}}}C^{*}\big({\mathcal{K}}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}\big)(G,f,e)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in E_{G}^{{\sf non-loop}}}t^{|E_{G}|-1}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(G,f)=|E_{G}^{{\sf non-loop}}|\cdot t^{|E_{G}|-1}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(G,f).

The case when
e
is a loop edge is similar, and it contributes in the
integrand by

|E_{G}^{{\sf loop}}|\cdot t^{|E_{G}|-1}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(G,f).

Putting these calculations together we see that the integrand gives

|E_{G}|\cdot t^{|E_{G}|-1}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(G,f)
, which matches exactly
with the differential equation satisfied by
{\mathcal{K}}_{m}^{\prime}(t)
. This finishes
the proof of Theorem
7.1
.

8
The Koszul trivializing morphism
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}

In (
4.2
) we defined a Koszul-type resolution
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}

of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}
, given by

\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}=\bigoplus_{k\geq 1,l\geq 0}\Hom^{\sf c}\left(\Sym^{k}(L_{+}[1]),\Sym^{l}(L_{-})\right)\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt.

In order to simplify notation we shall write
L_{k,l}
for the space

L_{k,l}=\Hom^{\sf c}\left(\Sym^{k}(L_{+}[1]),\Sym^{l}(L_{-})\right).

Consider the trivialization
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
of
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
, which is the
DGLA whose underlying
\Z/2\Z
-graded vector space is the same as that
of
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
, but with differential
b+uB+\iota
and with zero Lie
bracket. The purpose this section is to construct an
L_{\infty}

quasi-isomorphism

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}:\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{{\mathsf{triv}}}

from the data of a chain-level splitting
S:L\rightarrow L_{+}
of the Hodge
filtration on the cyclic homology of
A
.

An important technical difference from the previous section is that we
will need to assume that
S
is symplectic, or at least homologically
symplectic (see Definition
7.3
). The construction of

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}
will be similar to the one in the previous section in the sense
that the component maps
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}\;(m\geq 1)
will be written as
summations over certain graphs, the partially directed graphs that
we introduce below.

8.1
More first order trivializations

We need to construct two more bounding homotopies, in a way similar to
the construction of the homotopy
H
in
Proposition
7.5
, but for the operators
\Theta
and

\delta
.

Recall that in (
4.2
) we introduced the even operator

\Theta:L_{-}\rightarrow L_{+}[1]
given by

\Theta(\alpha)=B(\alpha_{0})

for
\alpha=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{-1}u^{-1}+\cdots
. Let

S:L\rightarrow L_{+}
be a chain-level splitting. Define the odd map

F:L_{-}\rightarrow L_{+}[1]
by

F(\beta)=-u^{-1}\cdot S\,\tau_{\geq 1}\,R(\beta).

(This is well defined because of the
\tau_{\geq 1}
operator in the middle.)
Explicitly, for
i\geq 0
,
j\geq 0
, the component
F_{i,j}
of
F
that
maps
L\cdot u^{-i}
to
L\cdot u^{j}
is given by

F_{i,j}(x\cdot u^{-i})=-\sum_{l=0}^{j}S_{l}R_{i+j+1-l}\,x\cdot u^{j}.

Proposition 8.1
.

The map
F:L_{-}\rightarrow L_{+}[1]
is a bounding homotopy of
\Theta
:

[\eth,F]=-(b+uB)F+F(b+uB)=\Theta.

(Here the differential of
L_{+}[1]
is
-(b+uB)
due to the shift.)

Moreover, the map
F
is compatible with the homotopy operator
H

constructed in Proposition
7.5
, in the sense that for

\alpha,\beta\in L_{-}
we have

\iota(\alpha)(F\beta)=H(\beta,\alpha),

where
\iota:L_{-}\rightarrow\Hom^{\sf c}(L_{+}[1],\mathbb{K})
is given by

\iota(\alpha)(\beta)=(-1)^{|\alpha|^{\prime}}\langle\alpha,u\cdot\beta\rangle_{\sf res}

as in (
4.2
).

Proof.

The proof is by direct computation.
∎

We use the homotopy operator
F
to define an even operator

\{-,-\}_{\hbar}^{F}:\Sym^{2}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1])\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]

using a definition similar to that of
\{-,-\}_{\hbar}

in (
4.2
). More precisely, in the definition of the
twisted composition map
\circ_{r}
, instead of using
r
copies of

\Theta
, we replace one
\Theta
with an
F
. This yields

\displaystyle(\Psi\circ^{F}_{r}\Phi)(\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k_{1}+k_{2}-r})

\displaystyle=\sum_{P,Q}\sum_{I,J}\epsilon_{I,J}\epsilon_{P,Q}(-1)^{\star}\Phi(\beta_{I})_{P}\cdot\Psi\big(F^{[r]}(\Phi(\beta_{I})_{Q})\otimes\beta_{J}\big)

\displaystyle F^{[r]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\Theta^{j-1}\otimes F\otimes\Theta^{r-j}

Here
\star=|\Psi||\Phi(\beta_{I})_{P}|+|\Psi|+|\Phi(\beta_{I})_{P}|
. Its
sign diagram is depicted as

\includegraphics[scale={.5}]{png/homotopy-bracket.png}.

We use the convention that the blue directed edge represents the
homotopy operator
F
. Since
F:L_{-}\rightarrow L_{+}[1]
is odd there is an
extra red line associated to it. This explains the sign difference
between
\circ^{F}_{r}
and
\circ_{r}
. Symmetrizing
\circ^{F}_{r}
and
summing over
r
as before we obtain the desired map

\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{\hbar}^{F}=\sum_{r\geq 1}\{\Psi,\Phi\}_{r}\cdot\hbar^{r-1}

where

\{\Psi,\Phi\}^{F}_{r}=(-1)^{|\Psi|}\big(\Psi\circ^{F}_{r}\Phi-(-1)^{|\Psi||\Phi|}\Phi\circ^{F}_{r}\Psi\big).

If the splitting
S
is only homologically symplectic and not
symplectic, we shall need to define a map

\{-,-\}_{\hbar}^{\delta}:\Sym^{2}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1])\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]

that will fix the fact that
H
is only homotopy-symmetric. The
definition of the map
\{-,-\}_{\hbar}^{\delta}
is exactly the same as
that of
\{-,-\}_{\hbar}^{F}
, but replacing in the above construction
F

with the operator
\delta
of Lemma
7.7
.

8.2
Partially directed graphs

Before we write down the formula for the quasi-isomorphism
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}
we
need to introduce a new type of graphs which we will call partially
directed graphs. The components of
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}
will be written as sums
over these graphs.

Definition 8.2
.

A partially directed graph of type
(g,k,l)
is given by a quadruple

\mathbb{G}=(G,L_{G}^{\sf in}\coprod L_{G}^{\sf out},E^{\mathsf{dir}},T)
consisting of the
following data:

–

A labeled graph
G
of type
(g,k+l)
.

–

A decomposition

L_{G}=L_{G}^{\sf in}\coprod L_{G}^{\sf out}

of the set of leaves

L_{G}
such that
|L_{G}^{\sf in}|=k
and
|L_{G}^{\sf out}|=l
. Leaves in

L_{G}^{\sf in}
will be called incoming, while leaves in
L_{G}^{\sf out}
will be called outgoing.

–

A subset
E^{\mathsf{dir}}\subset E_{G}
of edges of
G
whose
elements are called directed edges, and a direction is chosen on
them. Edges in
E_{G}-E^{\mathsf{dir}}
are called undirected.

–

A spanning tree
T\subset E_{G}
of the graph
G
.

We require the following properties to hold:

–

There is no directed loop.

–

Each vertex has at least one incoming half-edge.

A partially directed graph is called stable if the underlying
labeled graph is.

An isomorphism
\varphi:\mathbb{G}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{2}
of partially directed graphs
consists of an isomorphism of underlying labeled graphs
G_{1}
and

G_{2}
which preserves the directions of leaves and edges, and such that

\varphi(T_{1})=T_{2}
. Denote by
\Gamma(g,k,l)
the set of isomorphism
classes of partially directed graphs of type
(g,k,l)
and by

\Gamma\!\left(\hskip-2.33336pt\hbox{$\left(\hbox{$g,k,l$}\right)$}\hskip-2.33336pt\right)\!\hskip 0.66666pt
the subset of those that are stable. A marking
of a partially directed graph
\mathbb{G}
is a bijection

f:\{1,\cdots,m\}\rightarrow V_{G}
. Denote by
\widetilde{\Gamma(g,k,l)}_{m}

the set of isomorphism classes of marked partially directed graphs
with
m
vertices, and by
\widetilde{\Gamma\!\left(\hskip-2.33336pt\hbox{$\left(\hbox{$g,k,l$}\right)$}\hskip-2.33336pt\right)\!\hskip 0.66666pt}_{m}
the
corresponding set of marked stable partially directed graphs.

8.3
The weight of a partially directed graph

We need to associate a rational number
\wt(\mathbb{G})\in\mathbb{Q}
to a
partially directed graph
\mathbb{G}
. This will be used in the explicit formula for

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}:\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
.

Let
e\in T
be an edge from the spanning tree
T
. We call it
contractible if the graph obtained from
\mathbb{G}
by contracting all the
directed edges connecting the two end points of
e
is again a valid
partially directed graph. Denote the resulting partially directed
graph by
\mathbb{G}/e
. Denote by
T^{\sf contr}\subset T
the set of
contractible edges, and by
E_{G}^{\sf non-loop}
the set of non-loop
edges of
G
.

We inductively define
\wt(\mathbb{G})
as follows:

•

If
|T|=\emptyset
(equivalently, if
\mathbb{G}
only has one vertex)
then
\wt(\mathbb{G})=1
.

•

In general we set

\wt(\mathbb{G})=\frac{1}{|E_{G}^{\sf non-loop}|}\sum_{e\in T^{\sf contr}}\wt(\mathbb{G}/e).

An immediate corollary of this definition is that
\wt(\mathbb{G})=1
if
g(\mathbb{G})=0
.
Indeed, in the genus zero case, since all edges in
T
are
contractible and
T=E_{G}^{\sf non-loop}
, it is easy to prove that

\wt(\mathbb{G})=1
by induction.

8.4
The

L_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphism
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}

As in (
7.3
) we shall construct the
L_{\infty}

quasi-isomorphism

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}:\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}

by integrating a DGLA structure on the tensor product

\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\otimes\Omega_{[0,1]}^{\bullet}
. On this tensor product the
differential and Lie bracket are given by

b+uB+\hbar t\Delta+\iota+d_{DR}+\hbar\Delta^{H}dt

and

\sum_{r\geq 1}t^{r}\{-,-\}_{r}\hbar^{r-1}+\sum_{r\geq 1}t^{r-1}\{-,-\}^{F}_{r}\hbar^{r-1}dt+\sum_{r\geq 1}t^{r-1}\{-,-\}^{\delta}_{r}\hbar^{r-1}dt,

respectively.

The result of this integration procedure (following Fukaya
[
Fuk10
]
)
gives an
L_{\infty}
morphism
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
, written in terms
of a graph sum similar to the one we used to define the morphism
{\mathcal{K}}

in (
7.3
). However, the weights used in Fukaya’s
formula are not directly computable.

Our approach is slightly different. We will define a pre-
L_{\infty}

morphism
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}:\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
by a sum over graphs with
explicitly computable weights, and we show that it agrees with the
morphism defined by Fukaya. From this it will follow that the
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}

we have defined is indeed an
L_{\infty}
morphism.

For each
m\geq 1
we need to construct a map

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}:\Sym^{m}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1])\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}[1].

As before, the map
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}
is defined as a sum over graphs. This
time, however, the sum is over partially directed graphs. Let

(\mathbb{G},f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,k,l)}_{m}
be a marked partially directed
graph with
m
vertices. We proceed to define a
\mathbb{K}
-linear map

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{(\mathbb{G},f)}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}L_{k_{i},l_{i}}[1]\cdot\hbar^{g_{i}}\rightarrow L_{k,l}[1]\cdot\hbar^{g}.

This map is only non-zero when the combinatorial types match at each
vertex, i.e., for each
1\leq i\leq m
, we have
k_{i}=k_{f(i)}
,

l_{i}=l_{f(i)}
, and
g_{i}=g_{f(i)}
. When this is the case, the
result

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{(\mathbb{G},f)}(\gamma_{1}\cdot\hbar^{g_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\cdot\hbar^{g_{m}})
is defined as follows:

•

The tensor
\widetilde{\gamma_{i}}
is assigned to the vertex
f(i)
. Here,
for a map
\gamma_{i}\in L_{k_{i},l_{i}}
, its de-symmetrization

\widetilde{\gamma_{i}}\in\Hom^{\sf c}\left((L_{+}[1])^{\otimes k_{i}},L_{-}^{\otimes l_{i}}\right)

is the composition

(L_{+}[1])^{\otimes k_{i}}\rightarrow\Sym^{k_{i}}(L_{+}[1])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sym^{l_{i}}(L_{-})\rightarrow L_{-}^{\otimes l_{i}}.

•

The homotopy operator
F
in
Proposition
8.1
is assigned to each directed edge in the
spanning tree
T
; the operator
\Theta

in (
4.2
) is associated to the other directed edges.

•

The operator
\delta
of Lemma
7.7
labels each
undirected edge in the spanning tree
T
; the homotopy
operator
H^{{\sf sym}}
in Proposition
7.5
is assigned
to the other undirected edges.

•

The result
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{(\mathbb{G},f)}(\gamma_{1}\cdot\hbar^{g_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\cdot\hbar^{g_{m}})
is obtained by
composition along
\mathbb{G}
, with signs given by sign diagrams using
formulas like in the definition of
\{-,-\}_{\hbar}^{F}
.

Assembling all these maps together we obtain a graph sum

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}(\gamma_{1}\cdot\hbar^{g_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\cdot\hbar^{g_{m}})=\sum_{(\mathbb{G},f)}\frac{\wt(\mathbb{G})}{\Aut(\mathbb{G},f)}{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{(\mathbb{G},f)}(\gamma_{1}\cdot\hbar^{g_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\cdot\hbar^{g_{m}}).

The summation above is over all marked partially directed graphs with

m
vertices, for various
(g,k,l)
.

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
S
be a homologically symplectic chain-level splitting. Then the
map
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}
defined in terms of
S
is a quasi-isomorphism of DGLAs

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}:\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
. Moreover, we
have a homotopy commutative diagram of DGLAs

\begin{CD}{\mathfrak{h}}^{+}_{A}@>{\epsilon}>{}>\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\\
@V{{\mathcal{K}}}V{}V@V{}V{{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}}V\\
{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+,{\mathsf{triv}}}@>{\epsilon}>{}>\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}.\end{CD}

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Theorem
7.1
. To set it up,
define a family of pre-
L_{\infty}
morphisms

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}(t):\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}(t)\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
by setting

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}(t)(\gamma_{1}\cdot\hbar^{g_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\cdot\hbar^{g_{m}})=\sum_{(\mathbb{G},f)}\frac{t^{|E_{G}|}\wt(\mathbb{G})}{\Aut(\mathbb{G},f)}{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{(\mathbb{G},f)}(\gamma_{1}\cdot\hbar^{g_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\cdot\hbar^{g_{m}}),

with the weight defined in (
8.3
). Observe that by
definition we have
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}(1)={\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}
, and that
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}(0)
is the
identity morphism of
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}
.

There are three operators used in the earlier definition of the DGLA structure
on

\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\otimes\Omega_{[0,1]}^{\bullet},

namely
\Delta^{H^{\sf sym}}
,

\sum_{r\geq 1}t^{r-1}\cdot\{-,-\}^{F}_{r}\cdot\hbar^{r-1}
, and

\sum_{r\geq 1}t^{r-1}\cdot\{-,-\}^{\delta}_{r}\cdot\hbar^{r-1}
.
Using the bar construction for DGLAs we extend these
operators as coderivations, to obtain

\widehat{\nu}_{1}(t),\,\widehat{\nu}_{2}^{F}(t),\,\widehat{\nu}_{3}^{\delta}(t):\Sym(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1])\rightarrow\Sym(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}[1]).

The integration procedure described in
[
Fuk10
]

produces a family of
L_{\infty}
morphisms uniquely determined by a
differential equation. We shall prove that the pre-
L_{\infty}
morphism

{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}(t)
defined above satisfies the same
differential equation, which will prove that
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}(t)
is
indeed an
L_{\infty}
morphism.

This differential equation is given by

\frac{d}{dt}{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}(t)=\hbar{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}(t)\widehat{\nu}_{1}+{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m-1}(t)\widehat{\nu}^{F}_{2}(t)+{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m-1}(t)\widehat{\nu}^{\delta}_{2}(t).

To prove that the
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}
’s satisfy the above identity, observe that
for a marked partially directed graph
(\mathbb{G},f)
, the
t
-dependence is
simply by
t^{|E_{G}|}
. Hence the left hand side equals to

\int_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}|E_{G}|\cdot t^{|E_{G}|-1}\cdot\wt\,\cdot\,{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}\,d\mu,

where
\mathbb{D}_{m}
denotes the groupoid of marked
partially directed graphs with
m
vertices, and as in the proof of
Theorem
7.1
we make use of groupoid integrals developed
in
[
Fio06
]
.

Thus our goal is to show that the right hand side of the differential
equation is equal to the above groupoid integral. We first analyze
the first term
\hbar{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}(t)\widehat{\nu}_{1}
on the right hand
side of the differential equation. According to the inductive
definition of the weights, we observe that
\wt(\mathbb{G})=\wt(\mathbb{G}/e)
if

e
is a loop edge. With this fact in mind, arguing the same way as in
the proof of Theorem
7.1
we obtain

\hbar{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}(t)\widehat{\nu}_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}|E_{G}^{{\sf loop}}|\cdot t^{|E_{G}|-1}\cdot\,\wt\,\cdot\,{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}\,d\mu.

In the case of non-loop edges, consider the following maps between
groupoids

\mathbb{D}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf F}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf C}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{D}_{m-1},

where the
middle groupoid
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{m}
has objects consisting of triples

(\mathbb{G},f,e)
with
e\in T
a contractible edge in the spanning tree of

\mathbb{G}
. The map
{\sf F}
is the forgetful map
(\mathbb{G},f,e)\mapsto(\mathbb{G},f)
, while the map
{{\sf C}}
is the contraction map

(\mathbb{G},f,e)\mapsto(\mathbb{G}/e,f^{\prime})
.

We apply the basic properties of groupoid integrals to simplify the
second and the third terms on the right hand side of
the differential equation. If we denote
\nu_{2}(t)
the sum

\big(\nu^{F}_{2}(t)+\nu^{\delta}_{2}(t)\big)
, then the second and
the third terms are given, as in the proof of Theorem
7.1
, by
[
Fio06
]

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{D}_{m-1}}\wt\cdot{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}(t)d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{m}}{\sf C}^{*}\big(\wt\cdot{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}(t)\big)d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}{\sf F}_{*}{\sf C}^{*}\big(\wt\cdot{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}(t)\big)d\mu.

The vector-valued function

{\sf F}_{*}{\sf C}^{*}\big(\wt\cdot{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}(t)\big)
is computed as

\displaystyle{\sf F}_{*}{\sf C}^{*}

\displaystyle\big(\wt\cdot{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}(t)\big)(\mathbb{G},f)=\sum_{e\in T^{\sf contr}}{\sf C}^{*}\big(\wt\cdot{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m-1}(t)\nu_{2}(t)\big)(\mathbb{G},f,e)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in T^{\sf contr}}\wt(\mathbb{G}/e)\cdot t^{|E_{G}|-1}\cdot{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}(\mathbb{G},f)=|E_{G}^{\sf non-loop}|\cdot\wt(\mathbb{G})\cdot t^{|E_{G}|-1}\cdot{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}(\mathbb{G},f),

where the last equality follows from the inductive definition of

\wt
.

The above expression and the contribution from the loop edges in
add up precisely to
|E_{G}|\cdot t^{|E_{G}|-1}\cdot\wt(\mathbb{G})\cdot{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}(\mathbb{G},f)
. This proves that
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}
’s satisfy Fukaya’s differential
equation, and thus
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}
is an
L_{\infty}
morphism.

For the second part of the theorem, it follows from a general fact on
maps of pseudo-isotopies
[
Tu14
, Theorem 2.7]
. Indeed observe
that the map
\epsilon
lifts to a map between pseudo-isotopies:

\epsilon:{\mathfrak{h}}^{+}_{A}\otimes\Omega_{[0,1]}^{*}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}\otimes\Omega_{[0,1]}^{*}.

This induces the desired homotopy commutative diagram after
integrating both sides.
∎

9
Feynman sum formulas for CEI

In this section we use the
L_{\infty}
-morphism
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}
of
Theorem
8.3
in order to derive explicit formulas for the
categorical invariants
F_{g,n}^{A,s}
. These formulas are given as
summations over partially directed stable graphs, with vertices
labeled by the tensors
\widehat{\beta}^{A}_{g,k,l}
defined using combinatorial
string vertices in (
6.2
).

9.1
Expressing CEI in terms of

\widehat{\beta}^{A}

Let
s:H\rightarrow H_{*}(L_{+})
be a splitting of the Hodge filtration,
see Definition
6.9
. As in the previous section we fix a
chain-level splitting
S:(L,b)\rightarrow(L\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$u$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt,b+uB)
that lifts

s
, of the form

S=\id+S_{1}u+S_{2}u^{2}+\cdots,\;\;S_{j}\in\End(L).

As in Section
7
we also have its inverse operator

R=S^{-1}
.

Denote by
L^{\sf Triv}=(L,b)
the same underlying chain complex as

L
, but endowed with trivial circle action. The chain map
S
induces
an isomorphism
L^{\sf Triv}_{-}\rightarrow L_{-}
, further inducing an
isomorphism of DGLAs which we still denote by

S:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\sf Triv}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}.

Here the differential on the left hand side is just
b
while on the
right hand side it is
b+uB
; both sides have zero Lie
bracket. Analogously we also have an inverse isomorphism of DGLAs

R:{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\sf Triv}.

We also have the corresponding maps of Koszul resolutions

\widehat{R}:\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\sf Triv}\;\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\;\widehat{S}:\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\sf Triv}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}.

For example
\widehat{S}
pre-composes with
R
at inputs and
post-composes with
S
at outputs.

Consider the following commutative diagram of DGLAs
4
4

4

The left
square is only homotopy commutative by Theorem
8.3
.
:

\begin{CD}{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+}@>{{\mathcal{K}}}>{}>{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{{\sf triv},+}@>{R}>{}>{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{{\sf Triv},+}\\
@V{\bar{\iota}}V{}V@V{\bar{\iota}}V{}V@V{\bar{\iota}}V{}V\\
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}@>{{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}}>{}>\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}}@>{\widehat{R}}>{}>\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\sf Triv}.\end{CD}

It induces a commutative diagram of isomorphisms of the associated
Maurer-Cartan moduli spaces:

\begin{CD}{\mathsf{MC}}({\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{+})@>{{\mathcal{K}}_{*}}>{}>{\mathsf{MC}}({\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{{\mathsf{triv}},+})@>{R_{*}}>{}>{\mathsf{MC}}({\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{{\sf Triv},+})\\
@V{\bar{\iota}_{*}}V{}V@V{\bar{\iota}_{*}}V{}V@V{\bar{\iota}_{*}}V{}V\\
{\mathsf{MC}}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A})@>{{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{*}}>{}>{\mathsf{MC}}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\mathsf{triv}})@>{\widehat{R}_{*}}>{}>{\mathsf{MC}}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{\sf Triv}).\end{CD}

The categorical enumerative invariant
F^{A,s}
of
Definition
6.12
lives in the upper-right corner space

{\mathsf{MC}}({\mathfrak{h}}_{A}^{{\sf Triv},+})
, while the Maurer-Cartan element
\widehat{\beta}^{A}

lives inside the lower-left corner
{\mathsf{MC}}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A})
. Our goal is to write
down a formula for
F^{A,s}
in terms of
\widehat{\beta}^{A}
, as the latter is
computable using combinatorial string vertices from
Definition
5.2
. Using Theorem
8.3

it follows that

\bar{\iota}_{*}F^{A,s}=\widehat{R}_{*}{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{*}\widehat{\beta}^{A}.

This yields an explicit formula for the categorical enumerative
invariants
F^{A,s}_{g,n}
as a Feynman sum over partially directed
stable graphs, using the formula for
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}
described
in (
8.4
). The precise result is summarized
in the following theorem.

Theorem 9.1
.

For any
g\geq 0
,
n\geq 1
such that
2g-2+n>0
,
we have

\bar{\iota}(F_{g,n}^{A,s})=\sum_{m\geq 1}\sum_{\mathbb{G}\in\Gamma((g,1,n-1))_{m}}(-1)^{m-1}\frac{\wt(\mathbb{G})}{\Aut(\mathbb{G})}\prod_{v}{\sf Cont}(v)\prod_{e}{\sf Cont}(e)\prod_{l}{\sf Cont}(l)

as homology classes in
H_{*}\big(L_{1,n-1},b\big)
. In this formula
the contributions
{\sf Cont}(v)
,
{\sf Cont}(l)
and

{\sf Cont}(e)
associated with

\mathbb{G}=(G,L_{G}^{\sf in}\coprod L_{G}^{\sf out},E^{\mathsf{dir}},T)
are as follows:

–

At every vertex
v
we assign the tensor
\widehat{\beta}^{A}_{g(v),k(v),l(v)}
.

–

At incoming legs we assign the operator
S
defined above; at
outgoing legs we put the operator
R
.

–

For directed edges in the spanning tree
T
we assign the
homotopy operator
F
from Proposition
8.1
; for all
other directed edges we put the operator
\Theta
from (
4.2
).

–

For undirected edges in the spanning tree
T
we assign the
homotopy operator
\delta
in Lemma
7.7
; for all other
undirected edges we put the homotopy operator
H^{{\sf sym}}
from
Proposition
7.5
.

The total contribution
\prod_{v}{\sf Cont}(v)\prod_{e}{\sf Cont}(e)\prod_{l}{\sf Cont}(l)
is the composition
using the resulting sign diagram, which yields an element in

L_{1,n-1}
. The extra sign is due to the sign in the definition of

\{-,-\}_{\hbar}^{F}
in (
8.1
), since all the
\widehat{\beta}^{A}
’s
are odd elements of
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{A}
.

9.2
Explicit formulas for small
g
and

n

In this appendix we give explicit formulas for CEI of Euler
characteristic
\chi\geq-3
.

The formulas will be written in terms of partially directed graphs.
Our conventions when drawing such a graph
(G,L_{G}^{\sf in}\coprod L_{G}^{\sf out},E^{\sf dir},T)
are as follows:

–

we shall omit the genus decoration of a vertex if it is
clear from the combinatorics of the graph;

–

we shall omit the drawing of the spanning tree
T
if there
is a unique choice of it; otherwise, the spanning tree
T
will be
drawn in blue.

The formula for the
(0,1,2)
-component.

We begin
with the case when
g=0
and
n=3
. In this case there is a unique
partially directed stable graph. Thus we have

\bar{\iota}(F^{A,s}_{0,3})=\frac{1}{2}\includegraphics[trim=-28.45274pt 28.45274pt 0.0pt 0.0pt,scale={.25}]{png/012.png}

The coefficient
\frac{1}{2}
is due to the automorphism that switches
the two outputs in the stable graph.

The formula for the
(1,1,0)
-component.

In this case there are two stable graphs. The invariant
\bar{\iota}(F_{1,1}^{A,s})
is given by

The formula for the
(0,1,3)
-component.

In this
case the formula for
\bar{\iota}(F^{A,s}_{0,4})
is

The coefficient
\frac{1}{2}
appears due to the symmetry of the two
outgoing leaves on the right hand side of the stable graph.

The formula for the
(1,1,1)
-component.

Observe that in the first graph of the second line there are two
directed edges between the two vertices. This explains how tensors

\widehat{\beta}^{A}_{g,k,l}
with
k\geq 2
can contribute to the
categorical enumerative invariants. In the third graph of the second
line the spanning tree has an undirected edge in which case we assign
the homotopy operator
\delta
as in Lemma
7.7
. The
coefficient
-\frac{1}{2}
in the first three graphs of the bottom
line is deduced using the inductive definition of
weights (
8.3
):

\wt(\mathbb{G})=\frac{1}{|E_{G}^{\sf non-loop}|}=\frac{1}{2}.

The negative sign is because there are two vertices so

(-1)^{m-1}=-1
.

The formula for the
(0,1,4)
-component.

In this
case
\bar{\iota}(F_{0,5}^{A,s})
is given by

\includegraphics[scale={.3}]{png/014.png}.

In genus zero the weights of partially directed graphs are
1
,
see (
8.3
). Hence only the automorphism
factors show up. For example, the third graph in the bottom line has

\wt=1
and
|\Aut|=8
.

The formula for the
(1,1,2)
-component.

In this
case
\bar{\iota}(F_{1,3}^{A,s})
is given by

\includegraphics[scale={.5}]{png/112.png}.

The formula for the
(2,1,0)
-component.

We also
list the graph sum for
\bar{\iota}(F_{2,1}^{A,s})
:

\includegraphics[scale={.5}]{png/210.png}.

Appendix A
The integer-graded case

So far we have assumed that all our vector spaces were

\Z/2\Z
-graded, and we did not concern ourselves with even graded
shifts (as these do not affect signs). In this appendix we will
sketch how our results need to be modified in the
\Z
-graded case so
that all the maps involved will be of a well-defined homogeneous
degree. This allows us to obtain an analogue of the dimension axiom
of Gromov-Witten theory in this case.

The choices we make are as follows. The formal variables
u
and

\lambda
have homological degree
-2
. If the Calabi-Yau degree
(i.e., the homological degree of the cyclic pairing) of the algebra

A
is
d
, then
\hbar
has degree
-2+2d
. When the variable

\hbar
is used in the context of chains on moduli spaces of curves,

d
will be assumed to be zero (since the chain level operator giving
the Mukai pairing has degree zero), so
\deg\hbar=-2
in that
setting.

The standard Mukai pairing has homological degree zero

\langle\,-\,,\,-\,\rangle_{\sf Muk}:C_{*}(A)\otimes C_{*}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{K}.

Thus when viewed as an operator on
L=C_{*}(A)[d]
the Mukai pairing
has degree
-2d
.

With the above conventions the Weyl algebra is
\Z
-graded, because the
generators of the ideal defining it are of homogeneous degree
2d-2
.
This is the reason we choose the residue pairing to take the
coefficient of
u^{1}
and not
u^{-1}
.

We rewrite the definitions of
{\mathfrak{h}}
in (
4.1
) and of

\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
in (
4.2
) by adding even shifts:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}

\displaystyle=\Sym(L_{-})\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt[1-2d],

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k\geq 1,l}\Hom^{\mathsf{c}}\left(\Sym^{k}(L_{+}[1-2d]),\Sym^{l}L_{-}\right)[2-2d]\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt.

It is easy to check that with these gradings
{\mathfrak{h}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
are

\Z
-graded DGLAs.

Now assume that the splitting
s
, its chain-level lift
R
, and the
inverse
T
of
R
all preserve degrees. This implies that

–

H^{\Sym}:\Sym^{2}L_{-}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}
has degree
2-2d
in
Proposition
7.5
;

–

\Theta:L_{-}\rightarrow L_{+}[1-2d]
has degree

2-2d
in Proposition
8.1
;

–

F:L_{-}\rightarrow L_{+}[1-2d]
has degree
3-2d
in
Proposition
8.1
.

Using these facts it is easy to verify that the maps
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}_{m}

have degree zero, as desired.

We can now prove that the categorical enumerative invariants

F_{g,n}^{A,s}
satisfy the dimension axiom of Gromov-Witten theory
when the
A_{\infty}
-algebra is
\Z
-graded. More precisely we have the
following.

Theorem A.1
.

Assume that
A
is
\Z
-graded, of Calabi-Yau dimension

d
, and assume that the splitting
s
preserves degrees. Then

\deg F^{A,s}_{g,n}=2\big((d-3)(1-g)+n\big)

as an element of
\Sym^{n}H_{-}
.

Proof.

The element

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}=\sum_{g,k\geq 1,l}\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{g,k,l}\,\hbar^{g}\,\lambda^{2g-2+k+l}

is a solution of the Maurer-Cartan equation in
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
, so in
particular its (unshifted) degree is
-1
. Thus the degree of its component

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{g,k,l}
of genus
g
,
k
inputs and
l
outputs is

\deg\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{g,k,l}=6g-7+3k+2l.

Its image under
\rho^{A}
, as an element of
\Hom\left(\Sym^{k}(L_{+}[1]),\Sym^{l}(L_{-})\right)
will have degree

\deg\widehat{\beta}^{A}_{g,k,l}=(6g-7+3k+2l)+d(2-2g-2k).

See Theorem
6.1
for the provenance of the second term.

A simple inspection shows that in the
\Z
-graded version of
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}

(with the further shift by
2-2d+2dk-k
in its definition, and with

\hbar
of degree
2d-2
) the element

\widehat{\beta}^{A}_{g,k,l}\,\hbar^{g}\,\lambda^{2g-2+k+l}
has degree
-1
. We
conclude that
\widehat{\beta}^{A}
is a Maurer-Cartan element in the
\Z
-graded
DGLA
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
.

The splitting
s
is degree preserving, so we can find a chain-level
lift of it
R
that also preserves degrees. This implies that the
inverse
T
of
R
also preserves degrees, and hence the
L_{\infty}

morphism
{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}
has degree zero.

Therefore

F^{A,s}=\sum_{g,n}F^{A,s}_{g,n}\,\hbar^{g}\,\lambda^{2g-2+n},

as a Maurer-Cartan element of

{\mathfrak{h}}^{\sf Triv}=\Sym H_{-}\!\left[\hskip-1.66669pt\hbox{$\left[\hbox{$\hbar,\lambda$}\right]$}\hskip-1.66669pt\right]\!\hskip 0.66666pt[1-2d]
, also has
unshifted degree
-1
. When we consider
F^{A,s}_{g,n}
as an
element of
\Sym^{n}H_{-}
its degree is given by

\displaystyle\deg(F^{A,s}_{g,n})

\displaystyle=(-1)+(2d-1)-\deg(\hbar)\cdot g-\deg(\lambda)\cdot(2g-2+n)

\displaystyle=(2d-2)-(2d-2)g+2(2g-2+n)=2\big((d-3)(1-g)+n\big).

This indeed matches with the virtual dimension formula in
Gromov-Witten theory.
∎
